# 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the samples, such as their identity, composition, structure, concentration, purity, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as a source, a detector, a signal processor, a display, a sample holder, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as speed, cost, reliability, complexity, safety, etc.



# Analysis of Chloride Content, Hardness, and Alkalinity

- Chloride content is a measure of the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in a water sample. Chloride ions are derived from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage. Chloride content affects the taste, corrosion, and disinfection of water.
- Hardness is a measure of the amount of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions present in a water sample. These ions are derived from the dissolution of minerals, such as limestone, dolomite, and gypsum. Hardness affects the lathering, scaling, and softening of water.
- Alkalinity is a measure of the capacity of a water sample to neutralize acids. It is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonate (HCO3-), carbonate (CO32-), and hydroxide (OH-) ions. Alkalinity affects the pH, buffering, and coagulation of water.

- The analysis of chloride content, hardness, and alkalinity is important for the following reasons:
  - It helps to determine the suitability of water for various purposes, such as drinking, irrigation, industrial, and recreational uses.
  - It helps to monitor the quality of water sources and identify any changes or contamination.
  - It helps to design and operate water treatment processes, such as disinfection, softening, and coagulation.

- The analysis of chloride content, hardness, and alkalinity can be done by various methods, such as titration, colorimetry, ion chromatography, and spectrophotometry. Some of the common methods are:
  - Chloride content: Mohr's method, Volhard's method, Mercuric nitrate method, Argentometric method, etc.
  - Hardness: EDTA titration method, Soap titration method, Calmagite indicator method, etc.
  - Alkalinity: Phenolphthalein and methyl orange titration method, Bromocresol green and methyl red titration method, etc.



# Water

Water is a substance composed of the chemical elements hydrogen and oxygen and existing in gaseous, liquid, and solid states. It is one of the most plentiful and essential of compounds. A tasteless and odourless liquid at room temperature, it has the important ability to dissolve many other substances.

Some of the main properties and characteristics of water are:

- Water is a tiny molecule. It consists of three atoms: two of hydrogen and one of oxygen. Water molecules cling to each other because of a force called hydrogen bonding. It's the reason why water can do amazing things.
- Water is a shape-shifter. It exists in three states on Earth: liquid, gas, and solid. Liquid water is the most common form of water on Earth. Gas water is water vapor, which is invisible and forms clouds. Solid water is ice, which can take various shapes such as snowflakes, hail, and glaciers.
- Water is the liquid that makes life on Earth possible. As water cycles from the air to the land to the sea and back again, water shapes our planet — and nearly every aspect of our lives. All living things, from tiny cyanobacteria to giant blue whales, need water to survive.
- Water is an inorganic compound with the chemical formula H2O. It is a transparent, tasteless, odorless, and nearly colorless chemical substance, and it is the main constituent of Earth's hydrosphere and the fluids of all known living organisms (in which it acts as a solvent).
- Water has a high specific heat capacity, meaning it can absorb and release a lot of heat without changing its temperature much. This helps regulate the climate and the body temperature of living organisms.
- Water has a high surface tension, meaning it can form droplets and bubbles and resist external forces. This allows water to flow, stick to surfaces, and support some insects and plants.
- Water has a high dielectric constant, meaning it can reduce the electric force between charged particles. This makes water a good solvent for many substances, especially ionic compounds and polar molecules.
- Water can form hydrogen bonds with other molecules, such as DNA, proteins, and sugars. This helps stabilize the structure and function of these molecules, which are essential for life.

Water is also used for various purposes by humans, such as:

- Drinking, cooking, and washing
- Agriculture, irrigation, and livestock
- Industry, manufacturing, and energy
- Transportation, navigation, and recreation
- Sanitation, hygiene, and health
- Environmental protection, conservation, and restoration

Water is a valuable and limited resource that needs to be managed and used wisely. Some of the challenges and issues related to water are:

- Water scarcity, drought, and desertification
- Water pollution, contamination, and quality
- Water conflict, cooperation, and governance
- Water conservation, efficiency, and innovation
- Water education, awareness, and ethics

Water is a fascinating and vital topic that can be explored from many perspectives and disciplines. Water is also a source of inspiration, beauty, and wonder for many people. Water is life.



# 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a solution is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. pH = -log[H+]
- The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, lower than 7 being acidic, and higher than 7 being basic. The pH of pure water is 7 at 25°C.
- pH can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper, phenolphthalein, or universal indicator, which change color depending on the acidity or basicity of the solution. pH can also be measured using a pH meter, which is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the force per unit length required to stretch or break the surface of a liquid. Surface tension = F/L
- Surface tension causes liquids to form spherical droplets, to minimize their surface area. It also allows some insects, such as water striders, to walk on water without sinking.
- Surface tension can be measured using methods such as the drop weight method, the drop volume method, the pendant drop method, or the Wilhelmy plate method, which involve measuring the weight, volume, shape, or force of a liquid drop hanging from a needle, a tube, or a plate.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a fluid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a fluid. Viscosity = tau/gamma
- The viscosity of a fluid depends on its temperature, pressure, and composition. Generally, liquids have higher viscosity than gases, and viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and pressure.
- Viscosity can be measured using methods such as the capillary tube method, the falling ball method, the rotational viscometer method, or the oscillating disk method, which involve measuring the flow rate, the terminal velocity, the torque, or the frequency of a fluid passing through a narrow tube, a spherical ball, a rotating cylinder, or a vibrating disk.



# A Liquid

- A liquid is one of the four states of matter, along with solid, gas, and plasma.
- A liquid is a fluid that has a definite volume but no fixed shape. It can flow and take the shape of its container.
- A liquid is composed of molecules that are loosely bound by intermolecular forces, such as hydrogen bonds or van der Waals forces.
- A liquid can change its state to solid or gas by changing its temperature or pressure. The process of changing from liquid to solid is called freezing or solidification, and the process of changing from liquid to gas is called boiling or vaporization.
- A liquid has some properties that are different from solids and gases, such as viscosity, surface tension, capillarity, and vapor pressure. These properties depend on the type and strength of the intermolecular forces in the liquid.



# 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

- Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, and have a high molecular weight.
- Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, pharmaceuticals, cosmetics, etc.
- Resins can be classified into two main types: thermoplastic and thermosetting.
- Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be melted and reshaped by heating, such as polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, etc.
- Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo irreversible chemical reactions when heated, and form cross-linked networks that cannot be melted or reshaped, such as epoxy, phenolic, polyester, etc.
- Resins can be prepared by different methods, depending on their type and properties. Some of the common methods are:

  - Polymerization: This is the process of joining small molecules (monomers) to form large molecules (polymers) by covalent bonds. For example, ethylene can be polymerized to form polyethylene by using a catalyst and high pressure and temperature.
  - Condensation: This is the process of joining two or more molecules (monomers) to form a larger molecule (polymer) by eliminating a small molecule, such as water, ammonia, etc. For example, phenol and formaldehyde can be condensed to form phenolic resin by using an acid or a base as a catalyst and heating.
  - Copolymerization: This is the process of joining two or more different types of monomers to form a polymer that has properties of both monomers. For example, styrene and acrylonitrile can be copolymerized to form styrene-acrylonitrile (SAN) resin by using a free radical initiator and heating.
  - Modification: This is the process of changing the properties of a resin by adding or removing some functional groups, such as hydroxyl, carboxyl, amino, etc. For example, epoxy resin can be modified by adding a curing agent, such as an amine or an anhydride, to form a cross-linked network.



# Synthesis of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid with the formula C6H10O4. It is mainly used for the production of nylon, polyurethanes, and other polymers.
- The conventional method for the synthesis of adipic acid is the oxidation of a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol (KA oil) with nitric acid  .
- The oxidation of KA oil involves several steps and intermediate products, such as nitrosonium ion, nitrosocyclohexane, 6-hydroxyimino-6-nitrohexane, 6-oxo-6-nitrohexanoic acid, and glutaric acid.
- The overall reaction can be written as:

C6H10O + 4 HNO3 -> C6H10O4 + 4 NO2 + 2 H2O

- The nitric acid oxidation of KA oil produces nitrous oxide (N2O) as a by-product, which is a greenhouse gas and an ozone-depleting agent .
- Therefore, alternative methods for the synthesis of adipic acid from renewable and environmentally benign sources have been explored, such as the oxidation of glucose and its derivatives.
- The oxidation of glucose and its derivatives can be catalyzed by various metal-based nanocatalysts, such as Au, Pt, Pd, Cu, Co, Ni, and Mn.
- The advantages of using nanocatalysts are their high surface area, enhanced activity, selectivity, and stability.
- The challenges for the design of nanocatalysts for the synthesis of adipic acid from glucose and its derivatives are the control of the size, shape, composition, structure, and support of the nanoparticles, as well as the optimization of the reaction conditions.



# Acid and Paracetamol by Conventional and Green Route

- Paracetamol is a common painkiller used to treat aches and pain. It can also be used to reduce fever. Its chemical name is N-acetyl-p-aminophenol (PAR) or acetaminophen.
- The conventional route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves the following steps :
  - Nitration of phenol with nitric acid to form p-nitrophenol.
  - Reduction of p-nitrophenol with tin and hydrochloric acid to form p-aminophenol.
  - Acetylation of p-aminophenol with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol.
- The conventional route has some drawbacks, such as:
  - Low yield of p-nitrophenol due to unwanted ortho-nitration and oxidation of phenol.
  - Generation of toxic and corrosive by-products, such as nitrous acid, tin chloride and acetic acid.
  - Use of large amounts of solvents, such as water, ethanol and ether, which increase the waste and energy consumption.
- The green route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves the following steps  :
  - Direct amidation of hydroquinone with acetic anhydride and ammonium acetate in a solvent-free microwave-assisted reaction to form paracetamol.
  - Alternatively, direct acetylation of p-aminophenol with acetic acid and sodium acetate in a solvent-free microwave-assisted reaction to form paracetamol.
- The green route has some advantages, such as:
  - High yield and selectivity of paracetamol due to the use of microwave irradiation, which enhances the reaction rate and reduces the side reactions.
  - Elimination of the nitration and reduction steps, which simplifies the process and reduces the environmental impact.
  - Avoidance of the use of solvents, which minimizes the waste and energy consumption.



# LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

- An experiment is a scientific procedure that is carried out to test a hypothesis, observe a phenomenon, or measure a variable.
- Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more independent variables and measuring their effects on one or more dependent variables.
- Experiments can be classified into different types based on their design, purpose, or outcome. Some common types of experiments are:

  - Controlled experiments: These are experiments where only one independent variable is changed at a time, while all other variables are kept constant. The aim is to isolate the causal effect of the independent variable on the dependent variable. For example, a controlled experiment to test the effect of light intensity on plant growth would involve growing plants under different levels of light, while keeping other factors such as temperature, water, and soil constant.
  - Randomized experiments: These are experiments where the participants or units are randomly assigned to different groups or conditions. The aim is to reduce the bias or confounding factors that may affect the results. For example, a randomized experiment to test the effect of a new drug on blood pressure would involve randomly assigning patients to either receive the drug or a placebo, and measuring their blood pressure before and after the treatment.
  - Natural experiments: These are experiments where the independent variable is not manipulated by the experimenter, but rather changes naturally or due to some external factor. The aim is to exploit the natural variation in the independent variable to study its effect on the dependent variable. For example, a natural experiment to test the effect of air pollution on health would involve comparing the health outcomes of people living in areas with different levels of air pollution, while controlling for other factors such as age, income, and lifestyle.
  - Quasi-experiments: These are experiments where the independent variable is manipulated by the experimenter, but the participants or units are not randomly assigned to different groups or conditions. The aim is to approximate the causal effect of the independent variable on the dependent variable, while acknowledging the limitations and threats to validity. For example, a quasi-experiment to test the effect of a new curriculum on student achievement would involve implementing the curriculum in some schools or classes, while using the existing curriculum in others, and comparing the test scores of the students.
  - Field experiments: These are experiments that are conducted in a natural or real-world setting, rather than in a laboratory or controlled environment. The aim is to increase the external validity or generalizability of the results, while maintaining the internal validity or rigor of the design. For example, a field experiment to test the effect of a social media campaign on voter turnout would involve randomly assigning some voters to receive the campaign messages, while others do not, and measuring the actual voting behavior of the voters.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of verifying the accuracy and precision of an analytical instrument or device by comparing its measurements with a known standard of reference.
- Calibration ensures that the instrument or device is functioning properly and producing reliable and consistent results.
- Calibration is essential for maintaining the quality and validity of analytical data and for complying with regulatory requirements and standards.
- Calibration should be performed periodically, before and after each use, or whenever there is a change in the operating conditions or performance of the instrument or device.
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, and the calibration status should be indicated on the instrument or device.
- Calibration methods vary depending on the type and purpose of the instrument or device, but they generally involve the following steps:
  - Selecting an appropriate standard of reference that has a known or certified value and is traceable to a national or international standard.
  - Preparing the instrument or device for calibration by ensuring that it is clean, stable, and in good working condition.
  - Performing the calibration procedure by applying the standard of reference to the instrument or device and measuring its response.
  - Comparing the measured response with the expected or nominal value of the standard of reference and calculating the error or deviation.
  - Evaluating the error or deviation and determining if it is within the acceptable limits or tolerance of the instrument or device.
  - Adjusting or correcting the instrument or device if the error or deviation is outside the acceptable limits or tolerance.
  - Repeating the calibration procedure until the instrument or device meets the required accuracy and precision specifications.
  - Reporting and documenting the calibration results and updating the calibration status of the instrument or device.



# Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Method

- Hardness of water is a measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form stable complexes with metal ions, such as Ca2+ and Mg2+, in a 1:1 ratio.
- Eriochrome Black T is an indicator that changes color from wine red to blue when it binds to free metal ions in water.
- The principle of the EDTA method is to titrate a water sample with a standard EDTA solution until all the metal ions are complexed and the indicator changes color.
- The procedure for the EDTA method is as follows   :

  1. Take a sample volume of 20 ml (V ml) and transfer it to an Erlenmeyer flask.
  2. Dilute the sample to 40 ml by adding 20 ml of distilled water.
  3. Add 1 ml of ammonia buffer to adjust the pH to 10 ± 0.1.
  4. Add 1 or 2 drops of Eriochrome Black T indicator solution.
  5. Titrate the sample with a standard EDTA solution of known concentration (C mg/ml) until the wine red color just turns blue.
  6. Record the volume of EDTA solution used (T ml).
  7. Repeat the titration with a blank (distilled water) and a standard (hard water of known hardness) to check the accuracy and precision of the method.

- The calculation of the hardness of water by the EDTA method is based on the following formula   :

  - Hardness (mg/L as CaCO3) = (C × T × 1000) / V
  - Where C is the concentration of EDTA solution in mg/ml, T is the volume of EDTA solution used in ml, and V is the volume of water sample in ml.
  - The hardness can be reported as total hardness or calcium hardness, depending on the type of metal ions present in the water sample.



# Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids.
- Alkalinity is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality because it affects the pH, corrosion, and disinfection processes of water.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange.
- The endpoint of the titration is marked by a color change of the indicator from pink to colorless (phenolphthalein) or from yellow to orange (methyl orange).
- The amount of acid used to reach the endpoint is proportional to the alkalinity of the water sample.
- The alkalinity can be expressed in different units, such as milligrams per liter (mg/L) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3), milliequivalents per liter (meq/L), or degrees of hardness (dH).
- The alkalinity can be classified into different types, depending on the pH of the water sample and the indicator used:

  - Total alkalinity: the alkalinity measured by titrating the water sample with acid to the methyl orange endpoint (pH 4.5).
  - Phenolphthalein alkalinity: the alkalinity measured by titrating the water sample with acid to the phenolphthalein endpoint (pH 8.3).
  - Carbonate alkalinity: the alkalinity due to the presence of carbonates (CO3^2-^) and bicarbonates (HCO3^-^) in water. It is equal to the phenolphthalein alkalinity if the pH of the water sample is above 8.3, or half of the total alkalinity if the pH of the water sample is below 8.3.
  - Bicarbonate alkalinity: the alkalinity due to the presence of bicarbonates (HCO3^-^) in water. It is equal to the total alkalinity minus the carbonate alkalinity.
  - Hydroxide alkalinity: the alkalinity due to the presence of hydroxides (OH^-^) in water. It is equal to the phenolphthalein alkalinity minus the carbonate alkalinity if the pH of the water sample is above 8.3, or zero if the pH of the water sample is below 8.3.



# Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte) .
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H3O+] .
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added  .
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that responds to the hydrogen ion activity in the solution, and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential .
- The potential difference between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution, according to the Nernst equation .
- A plot of the pH of the solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a titration curve, which shows the changes in pH during the titration .
- The titration curve can be used to determine the endpoint or equivalence point of the titration, where the moles of titrant are equal to the moles of analyte .
- The shape and features of the titration curve depend on the type and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration .
- For example, a titration of a strong acid with a strong base produces a titration curve that has a steep rise near the equivalence point, which is at pH 7 .
- A titration of a weak acid with a strong base produces a titration curve that has a buffer region before the equivalence point, where the pH changes gradually, and a sharp rise at the equivalence point, which is above pH 7 .
- A titration of a weak base with a strong acid produces a titration curve that has a buffer region before the equivalence point, where the pH changes gradually, and a sharp drop at the equivalence point, which is below pH 7 .
- A titration of a polyprotic acid or base produces a titration curve that has multiple equivalence points, corresponding to the successive deprotonation or protonation of the analyte .
- The advantages of titrimetric method for pH determination are that it is accurate, precise, and applicable to a wide range of acid-base reactions .
- The disadvantages of titrimetric method for pH determination are that it requires a calibrated pH meter, a suitable indicator or endpoint detection method, and a standard titrant solution .



# Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that causes them to behave as if they have a thin elastic skin on their surface.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the du Noüy method.
- In this experiment, we will use the du Noüy method, which involves using a platinum ring attached to a torsion balance to measure the force required to detach the ring from the surface of the liquid.
- The apparatus consists of a torsion wire, a platinum ring, a pointer, a scale, a liquid container, and a leveling screw.
- The procedure is as follows:

  - Fill the container with the given liquid and place it on the leveling screw.
  - Adjust the leveling screw until the pointer is at zero on the scale.
  - Lower the ring into the liquid and then raise it slowly until it just touches the surface of the liquid.
  - Note the reading on the scale, which corresponds to the weight of the ring.
  - Raise the ring further until it detaches from the liquid surface, and note the reading on the scale, which corresponds to the weight of the ring plus the force of surface tension.
  - Repeat the steps for at least three times and take the average of the readings.
  - Calculate the surface tension of the liquid using the formula:

    - Surface tension = (F - W) / (4 * pi * R)

    - Where F is the force of surface tension, W is the weight of the ring, and R is the radius of the ring.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature and pressure of the fluid.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of the fluid depends on the pressure difference across the tube, the length and radius of the tube, and the viscosity of the fluid.
- The most common type of viscometer is the Ostwald viscometer, which consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a constant diameter capillary connecting them.
- The Ostwald viscometer works on the principle of Poiseuille's law, which states that the volumetric flow rate (Q) of a fluid through a capillary is given by:

Q = (πr^4 ΔP)/(8ηL)

where r is the radius of the capillary, ΔP is the pressure difference across the capillary, η is the viscosity of the fluid, and L is the length of the capillary.

- To determine the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer, the following steps are followed:

  - Fill the viscometer with the liquid to be tested and immerse it in a water bath at a constant temperature.
  - Suck some of the liquid into the upper bulb using a rubber tube and a suction pump, and then close the rubber tube with a clip.
  - Remove the clip and allow the liquid to flow through the capillary under the influence of gravity.
  - Measure the time (t) taken by the liquid to flow from one mark to another mark on the viscometer.
  - Repeat the experiment for at least three times and take the average time (t̅) as the flow time of the liquid.
  - Calculate the flow rate (Q) of the liquid using the formula:

Q = (πd^2 h)/(4t̅)

where d is the inner diameter of the capillary, h is the height difference between the two marks, and t̅ is the average flow time.

  - Repeat the above steps for a standard liquid of known viscosity (such as water or glycerin) and calculate its flow rate (Qs).
  - Calculate the relative viscosity (ηr) of the given liquid with respect to the standard liquid using the formula:

ηr = (Qs/Q)

  - Calculate the absolute viscosity (η) of the given liquid using the formula:

η = (ηr ηs)

where ηs is the viscosity of the standard liquid at the same temperature.

  - Record the observations and results in a tabular form and report the viscosity of the given liquid with appropriate units and significant figures.



# Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O.
- It is often used as a standard solution for titrations of reducing agents, such as oxalic acid, potassium permanganate, or potassium dichromate.
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using an external indicator such as starch or potassium ferricyanide.
- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate is:

(NH4)2Fe(SO4)2 + KMnO4 + H2SO4 -> Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + MnSO4 + NH4HSO4 + H2O

- The reaction is a redox reaction, in which iron(II) is oxidized to iron(III) and manganese(VII) is reduced to manganese(II).
- The end point of the titration is marked by the appearance of a faint pink color in the solution, due to the excess of potassium permanganate.
- The external indicator is used to detect the end point more accurately, as the color change is very faint and may be affected by the presence of other substances in the solution.
- Starch is used as an external indicator when the concentration of potassium permanganate is low, as it forms a blue-black complex with iodine, which is produced by the reaction of potassium permanganate and potassium iodide in acidic medium.
- Potassium ferricyanide is used as an external indicator when the concentration of potassium permanganate is high, as it forms a brown precipitate of Prussian blue with iron(III), which is produced by the oxidation of iron(II) by potassium permanganate.
- The procedure for the determination of the strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator is as follows:

1. Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate by dissolving a known mass of KMnO4 in distilled water and standardizing it against a primary standard, such as sodium oxalate or sodium thiosulfate.
2. Pipette out a known volume of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution into a conical flask and add a few drops of dilute sulfuric acid to acidify the solution.
3. Add a few drops of the external indicator, either starch or potassium ferricyanide, depending on the concentration of potassium permanganate.
4. Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution with the standard potassium permanganate solution, stirring constantly, until the end point is reached.
5. Note down the volume of potassium permanganate used and repeat the titration for two more times to get concordant readings.
6. Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution using the following formula:

Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (Volume of KMnO4 x Normality of KMnO4 x Equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate) / Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate

- The equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate is 196 g/mol, as one mole of it reacts with one mole of potassium permanganate.



# Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is as follows:

  $$K_2Cr_2O_7 + 6FeSO_4 + 7H_2SO_4 \rightarrow Cr_2(SO_4)_3 + 3K_2SO_4 + 6Fe_2(SO_4)_3 + 7H_2O$$

- The end point of this titration can be detected by the change in colour of the solution from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of ferric ions.
- This colour change is the internal indicator of the titration, and no external indicator is required.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

  $$\text{Strength of K}_2\text{Cr}_2\text{O}_7 = \frac{6 \times \text{Normality of FeSO}_4 \times \text{Volume of FeSO}_4}{\text{Volume of K}_2\text{Cr}_2\text{O}_7}$$

- The procedure of the titration is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of ferrous ammonium sulphate by dissolving 3.92 g of the salt in 250 mL of distilled water and adding 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Transfer 20 mL of this solution to a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Pipette out 10 mL of potassium dichromate solution of unknown strength into a burette and titrate it against the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution in the conical flask.
  - Note the initial and final burette readings and calculate the volume of potassium dichromate used.
  - Repeat the titration until concordant readings are obtained.
  - Calculate the strength of potassium dichromate using the formula given above.



# Determination of Available Chlorine in Bleaching Powder

Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2), calcium chloride (CaCl2) and calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2). It is commonly used as a disinfectant and a bleaching agent. The chlorine present in the bleaching powder gets reduced with time due to exposure to air and moisture. The amount of chlorine that can be liberated from the bleaching powder by the action of an acid is called the available chlorine. The percentage of available chlorine in a sample of bleaching powder can be determined by the iodometric method.

## Principle

The iodometric method is based on the reaction of chlorine with potassium iodide (KI) in an acidic medium to produce iodine (I2). The iodine is then titrated with sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) solution using starch as an indicator. The reactions are as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Cl}_2 + 2\text{KI} &\rightarrow 2\text{KCl} + \text{I}_2 \\
\text{I}_2 + 2\text{Na}_2\text{S}_2\text{O}_3 &\rightarrow \text{Na}_2\text{S}_4\text{O}_6 + 2\text{NaI}
\end{aligned}
$$

The amount of iodine liberated is equivalent to the amount of chlorine present in the bleaching powder sample. The percentage of available chlorine can be calculated from the volume and concentration of sodium thiosulfate solution used in the titration.

## Procedure

- Weigh accurately about 0.5 g of the bleaching powder sample and transfer it to a 250 mL conical flask.
- Add about 100 mL of distilled water and shake well to dissolve the sample.
- Filter the solution through a filter paper into another 250 mL conical flask.
- Pipette out 25 mL of the filtrate into a 250 mL conical flask and add 10 mL of 10% potassium iodide solution and 5 mL of 1 M sulfuric acid.
- Titrate the liberated iodine with 0.1 N sodium thiosulfate solution using a burette until the solution becomes pale yellow.
- Add a few drops of starch solution as an indicator and continue the titration until the blue color disappears.
- Note down the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used as the end point.
- Repeat the titration with another 25 mL aliquot of the filtrate and take the average volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used.

## Calculation

Let W be the weight of the bleaching powder sample in grams.

Let V be the average volume of 0.1 N sodium thiosulfate solution used in the titration in mL.

Let N be the normality of sodium thiosulfate solution.

The percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder sample can be calculated by the following formula:

$$
\text{% of available chlorine} = \frac{V \times N \times 35.5 \times 4 \times 100}{W \times 1000}
$$

where 35.5 is the equivalent weight of chlorine and 4 is the factor for 25 mL aliquot of 100 mL solution.



# Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters and wastewaters. It originates from various sources such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, domestic sewage, road salt, fertilizers, etc.
- High concentrations of chloride can have adverse effects on aquatic life, corrosion of pipes and fittings, taste and odour of drinking water, and salinity of soil and crops.
- Therefore, it is important to monitor and control the chloride content in water samples for environmental and health reasons.
- One of the common methods for determining the chloride content in water samples is the Mohr method, which is based on the precipitation and titration of chloride ions with silver nitrate solution using potassium chromate as an indicator.
- The principle of the Mohr method is as follows:

  - If water containing chlorides is titrated with silver nitrate solution, chlorides are precipitated as white silver chloride according to the reaction:

    `Cl- (aq) + Ag+ (aq) -> AgCl (s)`

  - Potassium chromate is used as an indicator, which supplies chromate ions that react with excess silver ions to form a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate according to the reaction:

    `CrO4 2- (aq) + 2Ag+ (aq) -> Ag2CrO4 (s)`

  - The end point of the titration is marked by the appearance of the first permanent reddish-brown precipitate, indicating that all the chloride ions have been precipitated and the silver nitrate solution is in excess.

- The procedure for the Mohr method is as follows  :

  - Take 50 mL of sample (V mL) and dilute to 100 mL with distilled water in a volumetric flask.
  - If the sample is highly coloured, add 3 mL of aluminium hydroxide and shake well, allow to settle, filter, wash and collect filtrate.
  - Bring the sample pH to 7-8 by adding acid or alkali if necessary. A pH meter or pH paper can be used to check the pH.
  - Add 1 mL of indicator (potassium chromate) to the sample in a conical flask.
  - Titrate the solution against standard silver nitrate solution (0.01 M or 0.02 M) from a burette until a reddish-brown precipitate is obtained. Record the volume of silver nitrate solution used (V1 mL).
  - Repeat the titration with two more aliquots of the sample and take the average volume of silver nitrate solution used (V2 mL).

- The calculation for the chloride content in water sample is as follows  :

  - The concentration of chloride ions in the sample (C mg/L) can be calculated from the following formula:

    `C = (V2 x N x 35.45 x 1000) / V`

    where V2 is the average volume of silver nitrate solution used (mL), N is the normality of silver nitrate solution, 35.45 is the equivalent weight of chloride ion, and V is the volume of sample taken (mL).

  - The percentage of chloride in the sample (% Cl) can be calculated from the following formula:

    `% Cl = (C x 100) / 1000`

    where C is the concentration of chloride ions in the sample (mg/L).

  - The results should be reported to the nearest 0.1 mg/L or 0.01 % as appropriate.



# Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde affects the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks.
- The steps involved in the preparation of phenol formaldehyde resin are :

  - Weighing the appropriate quantity of phenol and formaldehyde and mixing them in a reaction vessel.
  - Adding a catalyst, such as hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia, to initiate the polymerization reaction.
  - Heating the mixture to a suitable temperature and time to achieve the desired degree of polymerization and viscosity.
  - Cooling and neutralizing the resin with an alkali or an acid, depending on the catalyst used.
  - Filtering and drying the resin to remove any impurities and excess water.
  - Molding, curing, or modifying the resin for various applications.



# Preparation of Urea Formaldehyde (UF) Resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be carried out in one, two, or three steps, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin  .
- In the one-step process, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:1.5-2.5 and heated at 80-100°C with a base catalyst such as ammonia or pyridine. The reaction produces a low molecular weight resin that has a high formaldehyde content and a low viscosity. This resin is suitable for impregnating paper or fabric, but it has poor water resistance and high formaldehyde emission .
- In the two-step process, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:2-3 and heated at 80-100°C with a base catalyst in the first step. This produces monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea, which are intermediates that have one or two hydroxymethyl groups attached to the urea molecule. In the second step, the pH of the mixture is lowered by adding an acid catalyst such as sulfuric acid or oxalic acid, and the reaction is continued at 80-100°C. This results in the formation of a high molecular weight resin that has a low formaldehyde content and a high viscosity. This resin is suitable for making plywood, particle board, or MDF, but it still has moderate water resistance and formaldehyde emission .
- In the three-step process, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:2-3 and heated at 80-100°C with a base catalyst in the first step, as in the two-step process. In the second step, the pH of the mixture is adjusted to 6-8 by adding a buffer such as sodium bicarbonate, and the reaction is continued at 80-100°C. This produces a resin that has a moderate molecular weight and formaldehyde content, and a moderate viscosity. In the third step, the pH of the mixture is lowered by adding an acid catalyst, and the reaction is continued at 80-100°C. This results in the formation of a high molecular weight resin that has a low formaldehyde content and a high viscosity. This resin is suitable for making molded objects, such as buttons, knobs, or electrical switches, and it has good water resistance and low formaldehyde emission .
- The properties and applications of UF resin can be modified by adding various additives, such as fillers, extenders, modifiers, stabilizers, or curing agents. For example, melamine can be added to improve the water resistance and heat resistance of the resin, while phenol can be added to reduce the formaldehyde emission and increase the flexibility of the resin.

# 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- The conductance of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistance. It is a measure of how well the solution can carry an electric current.
- The conductance of a solution depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions it contains. The mobility of an ion is its ability to move through the solution under an electric potential gradient.
- The unit of conductance is siemens (S) or ohm^-1^. The unit of conductance per unit length is siemens per meter (S/m) or ohm^-1^ m^-1^.
- The conductance of a solution can be measured by using a special type of cell known as a conductivity cell and a Wheatstone bridge. A conductivity cell consists of two electrodes of known area and distance, immersed in the solution. A Wheatstone bridge is a circuit that can measure the resistance of an unknown resistor by comparing it with a known resistor.
- The resistance of the solution in the conductivity cell is given by:

  R = ρl/a

  where R is the resistance, ρ is the resistivity, l is the distance between the electrodes, and a is the area of the electrodes.

- The conductivity of the solution is the reciprocal of the resistivity, and is given by:

  κ = 1/ρ = a/Rl

  where κ is the conductivity.

- The cell constant, C, is the ratio of the distance and the area of the electrodes, and is given by:

  C = l/a

  The cell constant has the unit of m^-1^, and is a constant for a particular cell.

- The conductivity of the solution can be calculated from the cell constant and the resistance by using the formula:

  κ = C/R

  where R is the resistance measured by the Wheatstone bridge.

- The cell constant can be determined by measuring the resistance of a cell filled with a solution of known conductivity, such as 0.0200 M KCl. The conductivity of KCl at different temperatures can be found in standard tables.

- Once the cell constant is determined, the conductivity of any solution can be calculated from the experimental resistance by using the formula:

  κ = C/R

- The conductivity of a solution is related to the concentration of the ions by the equation:

  κ = Σciλi

  where ci is the concentration of the ith ion, and λi is the molar conductivity of the ith ion. The molar conductivity is the conductivity of a solution containing one mole of the ion per unit volume.

- The molar conductivity of an ion depends on its charge and mobility. The molar conductivity of an ion at infinite dilution, λi^0^, is the maximum value of the molar conductivity when the ion is completely dissociated and free from any interaction with other ions. The molar conductivity of an ion at any concentration, λi, can be calculated from the molar conductivity at infinite dilution by using the equation:

  λi = λi^0^ - k√ci

  where k is a constant that depends on the ion and the solvent.

- The molar conductivity of a solution at infinite dilution, Λ^0^, is the sum of the molar conductivities of the ions at infinite dilution, and is given by:

  Λ^0^ = Σνiλi^0^

  where νi is the stoichiometric coefficient of the ith ion in the electrolyte.

- The molar conductivity of a solution at any concentration, Λ, is the ratio of the conductivity and the concentration of the electrolyte, and is given by:

  Λ = κ/c

  where c is the concentration of the electrolyte.

- The molar conductivity of a solution at any concentration, Λ, can be calculated from the molar conductivity at infinite dilution, Λ^0^, by using the equation:

  Λ = Λ^0^ - K√c

  where K is a constant that depends on the electrolyte and the solvent.

- The degree of dissociation, α, of a weak electrolyte is the fraction of the electrolyte that is dissociated into ions in the solution. The degree of dissociation can be calculated from the molar conductivity of the solution at any concentration, Λ, and the molar conductivity at infinite dilution, Λ^0^, by using the equation:

  α = Λ/Λ^0^

  The degree of dissociation of a



# Determination of Rate Constant of Hydrolysis of Esters

- Hydrolysis of esters is the reverse reaction of esterification, in which an ester reacts with water to produce a carboxylic acid and an alcohol .
- The hydrolysis of esters can be catalyzed by either an acid or a base. In acidic hydrolysis, the ester is heated with a large excess of water containing a strong-acid catalyst. In basic hydrolysis, the ester is heated with a large excess of water containing a strong-base catalyst .
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on the nature of the ester, the temperature, the pH, and the concentration of the reactants .
- The rate law for the hydrolysis of esters can be written as:

  - For acidic hydrolysis: `rate = k[ester][H2O]`
  - For basic hydrolysis: `rate = k[ester][OH-]`

  where k is the rate constant, [ester] is the concentration of the ester, [H2O] is the concentration of water, and [OH-] is the concentration of hydroxide ions .
- The rate constant k can be determined experimentally by measuring the initial rate of hydrolysis of a known concentration of ester at a given temperature and pH. Alternatively, k can be determined by measuring the change in concentration of ester, acid, alcohol, or hydroxide ions over time and using the integrated rate law .
- For example, the rate constant for the acidic hydrolysis of ethyl acetate at 25°C and pH 4.0 is 1.78 x 10^-5 s^-1. This means that the rate of hydrolysis of 0.01 M ethyl acetate at these conditions is 1.78 x 10^-7 M/s .
- Another example is the rate constant for the basic hydrolysis of methyl benzoate at 25°C and pH 10.0 is 4.32 x 10^-3 s^-1. This means that the rate of hydrolysis of 0.01 M methyl benzoate at these conditions is 4.32 x 10^-5 M/s .
- The rate constant of hydrolysis of esters can be used to compare the reactivity of different esters, to calculate the equilibrium constant of the hydrolysis reaction, and to estimate the shelf life of ester-containing products .



# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Organic compounds are those that contain carbon atoms, usually bonded to hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, or halogens.
- The structure and properties of organic compounds are largely determined by the functional groups, which are specific groups of atoms or bonds that have characteristic chemical behavior.
- Functional groups can be classified into different categories, such as alcohols, phenols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, amines, etc.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds are important for the analysis and characterization of organic substances.
- There are various methods and tests for detecting and identifying the elements and functional groups in organic compounds, such as:

  - Solubility test: This test is based on the principle that different organic compounds have different solubility in water, dilute acid, or dilute base, depending on their polarity and functional groups. For example, alcohols and amines are soluble in water, carboxylic acids are soluble in dilute base, and phenols are soluble in dilute acid.
  - Lassaigne's test: This test is used to detect the presence of nitrogen, sulfur, and halogens in organic compounds. It involves heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube, which forms sodium cyanide, sodium sulfide, and sodium halides. These compounds are then extracted with water and tested with various reagents, such as ferrous sulfate, sodium nitroprusside, silver nitrate, etc.
  - 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine test: This test is used to detect the presence of carbonyl groups (aldehydes and ketones) in organic compounds. It involves adding a small amount of the organic compound to 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine reagent, which forms a yellow-orange-red precipitate of 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazone. The precipitate can be further purified and identified by its melting point and infrared spectrum.
  - Tollen's test: This test is used to distinguish between aldehydes and ketones. It involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a solution of silver nitrate and ammonia, which forms a complex called Tollen's reagent. If the compound is an aldehyde, it will reduce the silver ions to metallic silver, which forms a silver mirror on the inner surface of the test tube. If the compound is a ketone, no reaction will occur.
  - Fehling's test: This test is also used to distinguish between aldehydes and ketones. It involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a solution of copper sulfate and sodium hydroxide, which forms a complex called Fehling's reagent. If the compound is an aldehyde, it will reduce the copper ions to copper oxide, which forms a red precipitate. If the compound is a ketone, no reaction will occur.
  - Esterification test: This test is used to detect the presence of carboxylic acids in organic compounds. It involves heating the organic compound with an alcohol and a few drops of concentrated sulfuric acid, which acts as a catalyst. If the compound is a carboxylic acid, it will form an ester, which has a fruity smell. The ester can be further identified by its boiling point and infrared spectrum.
  - Nitrous acid test: This test is used to detect the presence of primary, secondary, and tertiary amines in organic compounds. It involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a solution of sodium nitrite and dilute hydrochloric acid, which forms nitrous acid. If the compound is a primary amine, it will form a diazonium salt, which is unstable and decomposes to form nitrogen gas and a phenol. If the compound is a secondary amine, it will form a nitrosamine, which is also unstable and decomposes to form nitrogen gas and an aldehyde or a ketone. If the compound is a tertiary amine, no reaction will occur.

# Course Outcomes:

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students should be able to do or know by the end of a course.
- Course outcomes are usually derived from the course objectives, which are the broad goals of the course.
- Course outcomes are specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART).
- Course outcomes help to align the course content, activities, assessments, and feedback with the intended learning outcomes of the course.
- Course outcomes help to communicate the expectations and standards of the course to the students, instructors, and other stakeholders.
- Course outcomes help to evaluate the effectiveness and quality of the course and identify areas for improvement.



# Upon completion of the course the student should be able to:

- Define and explain the basic concepts and principles of the subject matter.
- Apply the learned knowledge and skills to solve problems and perform tasks related to the course objectives.
- Analyze and evaluate information, arguments, and evidence from various sources and perspectives.
- Communicate effectively and appropriately in written and oral forms using the language and terminology of the discipline.
- Demonstrate ethical, professional, and social responsibility in academic and real-world contexts.
- Collaborate and cooperate with others in diverse and intercultural settings.
- Reflect on their own learning process and outcomes and identify areas for improvement and further development.



# Course Outcomes Bloom's

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students should be able to do or demonstrate after completing a course.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a framework for classifying educational objectives into six levels of cognitive complexity: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Course outcomes can be aligned with Bloom's taxonomy to ensure that they cover a range of cognitive skills and learning domains.
- Some examples of course outcomes and their corresponding Bloom's levels are:

  - Remember: Recall the basic concepts and principles of accounting.
  - Understand: Explain the difference between cash and accrual accounting methods.
  - Apply: Prepare journal entries and financial statements using the accounting equation.
  - Analyze: Interpret the financial ratios and indicators of a company's performance and liquidity.
  - Evaluate: Compare and contrast the advantages and disadvantages of different accounting standards and policies.
  - Create: Design and propose a budget plan for a business project.



# Level

- A level is a tool or device used to measure or indicate whether a surface is horizontal (level) or vertical (plumb).
- A level consists of a horizontal tube or vial filled with a liquid (usually water or alcohol) and an air bubble that moves when the level is tilted.
- A level is used to align or adjust objects such as shelves, picture frames, tables, walls, floors, etc. to ensure that they are parallel or perpendicular to the ground or another reference plane.
- A level can also be used to measure angles or slopes by reading the position of the bubble relative to the markings on the tube or vial.
- There are different types of levels, such as spirit levels, bubble levels, laser levels, digital levels, etc. that vary in size, shape, accuracy, and functionality.



# CO-1 Get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments. K3

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or identify the chemical, physical, or biological properties of a sample or a substance.
- Analytical instruments have various applications in different fields, such as pharmaceutical, food-processing, chemical, clinical, environmental, forensic, and life science research.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different types based on the principle or technique they use, such as spectroscopy, mass spectrometry, electrochemical analysis, thermal analysis, separation analysis, microscopy, and hybrid techniques.
- Some examples of analytical instruments are:

  - Refractometer: measures the refractive index of a liquid or a solid sample, which is related to its concentration, purity, or composition.
  - Spectrophotometer: measures the amount of light absorbed or transmitted by a sample at different wavelengths, which can be used to determine its concentration, structure, or identity.
  - Electrochemical instrument: measures the electrical properties of a sample, such as its potential, current, resistance, or conductivity, which can be used to determine its redox state, pH, or concentration of ions or molecules.
  - Calorimeter: measures the amount of heat released or absorbed by a sample during a chemical or physical process, which can be used to determine its enthalpy, entropy, or specific heat capacity.
  - Automatic density meter: measures the density or specific gravity of a liquid or a solid sample, which can be used to determine its purity, composition, or quality.
  - Conductivity meter: measures the ability of a sample to conduct electricity, which can be used to determine its concentration, salinity, or quality.
  - Colony counter: counts the number of microbial colonies on a culture plate, which can be used to determine the microbial load, growth rate, or viability of a sample.
  - Automatic titrator: performs a titration automatically by adding a titrant to a sample and measuring the change in a parameter, such as pH, color, or potential, which can be used to determine the concentration, acidity, or alkalinity of a sample.
  - Fiberscope: uses a flexible optical fiber to transmit light and images from a remote location, which can be used to inspect, diagnose, or treat inaccessible areas or organs.
  - Demagnetizer: removes the magnetic field from a sample or a device, which can be used to prevent interference, damage, or data loss.
  - Gas chromatography-mass spectrometry (GC-MS): combines gas chromatography and mass spectrometry to separate and identify the components of a complex mixture based on their volatility and mass-to-charge ratio.
  - High-performance liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (HPLC-MS): combines high-performance liquid chromatography and mass spectrometry to separate and identify the components of a complex mixture based on their polarity and mass-to-charge ratio.



# CO2: Molecular and System Properties

- Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a linear molecule with a bond length of 116.3 pm between the carbon and the oxygen atoms and a bond angle of 180 degrees.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero. It has four vibrational modes: symmetric stretching, asymmetric stretching, and two degenerate bending modes.
- CO2 is a gas at standard temperature and pressure, with a density of 1.977 kg/m3 and a molar mass of 44.0095 g/mol .
- CO2 can be dissolved in water and other solvents, forming weak carbonic acid (H2CO3) and bicarbonate (HCO3-) and carbonate (CO3 2-) ions. The solubility of CO2 in water decreases with increasing temperature and increases with increasing pressure.
- CO2 can also react with some bases, such as monoethanolamine (MEA), to form carbamate salts. These reactions are reversible and depend on the temperature, pressure, and concentration of the reactants .
- Surface tension is the energy, or work, required to increase the surface area of a liquid due to intermolecular forces. Surface tension is measured in units of N/m or J/m2.
- The surface tension of pure water at 20 °C is 72.8 mN/m. The surface tension of water decreases with increasing temperature and increases with increasing salinity.
- The surface tension of CO2-loaded aqueous solutions depends on the concentration of CO2, the type and concentration of the solvent, and the temperature. For example, the surface tension of aqueous MEA solutions decreases with increasing CO2 loading and increasing MEA mass ratio, and increases with increasing temperature .
- The surface tension of CO2-loaded aqueous solutions can be measured by various methods, such as the pendant drop method, the Wilhelmy plate method, or the du Noüy ring method . These methods involve measuring the force or shape of a liquid drop or a liquid film in contact with a solid or a gas.



# Viscosity, Conductance of Solution, Chloride and Iron Content in Water

## Viscosity

- Viscosity is the property of a fluid that measures its resistance to flow.
- Viscosity depends on the internal friction between the molecules of the fluid, which is affected by factors such as temperature, pressure, and composition.
- Viscosity is expressed by the formula:

    `η = F / (A * dv / dy)`

    where η is the viscosity, F is the force required to move a layer of fluid, A is the area of the layer, dv / dy is the velocity gradient between the layers.
- The SI unit of viscosity is pascal-second (Pa·s), which is equivalent to newton-second per square meter (N·s/m²) or kilogram per meter per second (kg/m·s).
- Some examples of fluids with different viscosities are:

    | Fluid | Viscosity (Pa·s) at 20°C |
    | ----- | ----------------------- |
    | Water | 0.001 |
    | Honey | 10 |
    | Glycerol | 1.5 |
    | Motor oil | 0.1 |
    | Air | 0.000018 |

- Viscosity can be classified into two types: dynamic viscosity and kinematic viscosity.
- Dynamic viscosity is the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate of a fluid, and it measures the force required to move a fluid.
- Kinematic viscosity is the ratio of the dynamic viscosity to the density of a fluid, and it measures the resistance of a fluid to flow under gravity.
- The SI unit of kinematic viscosity is square meter per second (m²/s), which is equivalent to stoke (St) or centistoke (cSt).
- Some examples of fluids with different kinematic viscosities are:

    | Fluid | Kinematic viscosity (m²/s) at 20°C |
    | ----- | -------------------------------- |
    | Water | 0.000001 |
    | Honey | 0.01 |
    | Glycerol | 0.001 |
    | Motor oil | 0.0001 |
    | Air | 0.000015 |

- Viscosity is an important property of fluids in many applications, such as lubrication, transport, mixing, and heat transfer.



# K3

- K3 is a type of surface in mathematics that is named after three mathematicians: Kummer, Kähler, and Kodaira.
- K3 surfaces are complex algebraic surfaces that have trivial canonical bundle and no holomorphic 1-forms.
- K3 surfaces are also Calabi-Yau manifolds, meaning that they have a Ricci-flat Kähler metric and a holonomy group that is a subgroup of SU(2).
- K3 surfaces have many interesting properties and applications in geometry, topology, number theory, and physics.
- Some examples of K3 surfaces are:

  - The quartic surface in P^3 defined by a homogeneous polynomial of degree 4.
  - The double cover of P^2 branched along a smooth sextic curve.
  - The Kummer surface, which is obtained by taking the quotient of a 2-torus by an involution and resolving the 16 singular points.
  - The resolution of the singularities of the Fermat quartic surface, which is defined by x^4 + y^4 + z^4 + w^4 = 0 in P^3.



# CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness and alkalinity are two important parameters of water quality that indicate the concentration of dissolved minerals and bases in water respectively.
- Hardness is caused by compounds of calcium and magnesium, and by a variety of other metals, such as iron, manganese, and zinc. Hard water can cause scaling, corrosion, and reduced efficiency of water appliances and heaters.
- Alkalinity measures the total amount of bases present in water that can neutralize acids and maintain a stable pH. Alkalinity is mainly contributed by bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions, which are associated with calcium and magnesium ions. Alkalinity can buffer the water against pH changes and protect aquatic life.
- Hardness and alkalinity often occur together, because some compounds can contribute both alkalinity and hardness ions, such as calcium carbonate and magnesium carbonate. However, hardness and alkalinity are not the same, and they can vary independently depending on the source and composition of water.
- To measure the hardness and alkalinity of water, titration methods are commonly used. Titration is a technique that involves adding a known amount of a solution with a known concentration (called titrant) to a sample with an unknown concentration (called analyte) until a color change or an endpoint is reached.
- To measure the hardness of water, a titrant of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) is used, which forms a complex with the hardness ions and removes them from the solution. The endpoint is indicated by a color change of a metal indicator, such as eriochrome black T, which turns from red to blue when all the hardness ions are complexed with EDTA.
- To measure the alkalinity of water, a titrant of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) is used, which reacts with the alkalinity ions and lowers the pH of the solution. The endpoint is indicated by a color change of a pH indicator, such as phenolphthalein or bromcresol green, which turns from pink or blue to colorless when the pH reaches a certain value.
- The amount of titrant used to reach the endpoint is proportional to the concentration of the analyte in the sample. By using a calibrated burette and a known volume of sample, the hardness and alkalinity of water can be calculated in units of milligrams per liter (mg/L) as calcium carbonate (CaCO3).



# CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound with a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. It is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol has antiseptic and disinfectant properties and is used in the manufacture of plastics, dyes, explosives, and drugs.

There are several methods of preparation of phenol, which can be classified into two categories: laboratory methods and industrial methods.

## Laboratory methods of preparation of phenol

- **From haloarenes:** Haloarenes are aromatic compounds with a halogen atom (-X) attached to a benzene ring. Examples of haloarenes are chlorobenzene, bromobenzene, and iodobenzene. When a haloarene is fused with sodium hydroxide at high temperature and pressure, sodium phenoxide is produced. Sodium phenoxide is then acidified with dilute acid or carbon dioxide to give phenol. This reaction is known as the Dow process or the Fries process  .

  - Example: Chlorobenzene + Sodium hydroxide → Sodium phenoxide + Sodium chloride
  - Sodium phenoxide + Dilute acid → Phenol + Water

- **From benzene sulphonic acid:** Benzene sulphonic acid is an aromatic compound with a sulphonic acid group (-SO3H) attached to a benzene ring. It is formed by the sulphonation of benzene with concentrated sulphuric acid. When the sodium salt of benzene sulphonic acid is fused with sodium hydroxide at high temperature, sodium phenoxide is produced. Sodium phenoxide is then acidified with dilute acid or carbon dioxide to give phenol  .

  - Example: Benzene + Concentrated sulphuric acid → Benzene sulphonic acid + Water
  - Sodium benzenesulphonate + Sodium hydroxide → Sodium phenoxide + Sodium sulphate
  - Sodium phenoxide + Dilute acid → Phenol + Water

- **From diazonium salts:** Diazonium salts are organic compounds with a diazonium group (-N2+) attached to an aromatic ring. They are formed by the reaction of an aromatic amine with nitrous acid. When a diazonium salt is treated with water, it undergoes hydrolysis to give phenol and nitrogen gas  .

  - Example: Aniline + Nitrous acid → Benzene diazonium chloride + Water
  - Benzene diazonium chloride + Water → Phenol + Nitrogen gas + Hydrochloric acid

## Industrial methods of preparation of phenol

- **From coal tar:** Coal tar is a dark brown liquid obtained as a by-product of the destructive distillation of coal. It contains various aromatic compounds, including phenol. Phenol is extracted from the middle oil fraction of coal tar by fractional distillation and purification .

- **From cumene:** Cumene is an organic compound with an isopropyl group (-CH(CH3)2) attached to a benzene ring. It is formed by the alkylation of benzene with propene. When cumene is oxidized by air, it forms cumene hydroperoxide, which is an unstable compound. Cumene hydroperoxide is then decomposed by heating or by acid catalysis to give phenol and acetone. This reaction is known as the cumene process or the Hock process  .

  - Example: Benzene + Propene → Cumene
  - Cumene + Air → Cumene hydroperoxide
  - Cumene hydroperoxide → Phenol + Acetone



# Formaldehyde & Urea Formaldehyde Resin, Adipic Acid and Paracetamol

- Formaldehyde is a colorless, flammable, strong-smelling chemical that is used in building materials and to produce many household products.
- Urea formaldehyde (UF) is a nontransparent thermosetting resin or polymer that is produced from urea and formaldehyde. It is used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin often shows the appearance of crystal domains, which are influenced by the synthesis conditions, such as pH, temperature, and molar ratio .
- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon. It can also be used as a modifier for UF resin to reduce the free formaldehyde content.
- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a medication used to treat pain and fever. It is one of the most commonly used drugs in the world. It works by inhibiting the synthesis of prostaglandins, which are involved in inflammation and pain sensation.



# K3

- K3 is a synthetic, artificially produced form of vitamin K that doesn’t occur naturally .
- K3 is also known as menadione .
- K3 may be converted into K2 in your liver.
- K3 is important for blood clotting and bone health.
- K3 has demonstrated anticancer and antibacterial properties in test-tube studies.
- K3 is not approved for human use by the FDA and may cause serious side effects such as liver damage, anemia, and oxidative stress .
- K3 is sometimes added to animal feed as a supplement .
- K3 is also the name of a schedule form for reporting a partner’s share of income, deductions, credits, etc. from international sources for U.S. tax purposes.



# CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction K3

- The rate constant of a reaction is a proportionality factor that relates the reaction rate to the concentrations of the reactants.
- The rate constant depends on the temperature, the activation energy, and the frequency factor of the reaction.
- The rate constant can have different units depending on the order of the reaction.
- For a termolecular reaction, such as A + B + C → products, the reaction rate is described by r=k3[A][B][C], where k3 is a termolecular rate constant.
- To estimate the rate constant of a termolecular reaction, one can use the Arrhenius equation: k3 = Ae^(-Ea/RT), where A is the frequency factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.
- Alternatively, one can use experimental data to plot the natural logarithm of the rate constant versus the inverse of the temperature, and obtain the slope and the intercept of the linear regression. The slope is equal to -Ea/R and the intercept is equal to ln A.
- If the reaction takes place in multiple steps, the overall rate constant can be expressed as a function of the individual rate constants of each step .
- For example, if a reaction takes place in three steps with rate constants k1, k2, and k3, the overall rate constant k can be calculated as k = k2k1k3/(k1 + k2 + k3).

## The topic is: How to write a formal email

- A formal email is a type of written communication that follows certain conventions of etiquette, grammar, and style.
- A formal email is usually used for professional or academic purposes, such as applying for a job, requesting information, or making a complaint.
- A formal email should have a clear and concise subject line that summarizes the main purpose of the email.
- A formal email should start with a polite salutation, such as "Dear Mr./Ms./Dr./Professor (last name)" or "To whom it may concern".
- A formal email should introduce yourself and state the reason for writing in the first paragraph.
- A formal email should provide relevant details and evidence to support your request, inquiry, or argument in the following paragraphs.
- A formal email should end with a courteous closing, such as "Sincerely", "Best regards", or "Thank you for your time and attention".
- A formal email should include your full name and contact information below the closing.
- A formal email should follow the standard rules of grammar, punctuation, and spelling, and avoid informal language, slang, or abbreviations.
- A formal email should be proofread and edited before sending to ensure clarity, accuracy, and professionalism.
