# Engineering Physics Lab

Engineering physics lab is a course that introduces students to the basic concepts and methods of experimental physics. The course aims to develop the skills of observation, measurement, analysis, and interpretation of physical phenomena. The course also exposes students to various instruments and techniques used in physics experiments.

Some of the topics covered in engineering physics lab are:

- Measurement of physical quantities and error analysis
- Newton's laws of motion and applications
- Conservation of energy and momentum
- Simple harmonic motion and resonance
- Waves and sound
- Optics and interference
- Electricity and magnetism
- Semiconductor devices and circuits

Some of the experiments performed in engineering physics lab are:

- Measurement of length, mass, and time using vernier calipers, screw gauge, and stopwatch
- Verification of Hooke's law and determination of Young's modulus of a wire
- Determination of the coefficient of friction and the angle of repose
- Verification of the principle of conservation of momentum using a ballistic pendulum
- Study of simple pendulum and determination of acceleration due to gravity
- Study of forced and damped oscillations using a spring-mass system
- Study of standing waves and determination of the speed of sound in air
- Study of diffraction and interference of light using a single slit, a double slit, and a diffraction grating
- Study of polarization of light using a polarizer and an analyzer
- Study of Ohm's law and Kirchhoff's laws using a voltmeter and an ammeter
- Study of the characteristics of a diode and a transistor using a multimeter and an oscilloscope
- Study of the logic gates and their applications using a breadboard and an LED

The engineering physics lab course helps students to:

- Understand the theoretical principles of physics and their experimental verification
- Develop the ability to design, conduct, and report physics experiments
- Learn the proper use and handling of various instruments and equipment
- Enhance the problem-solving and analytical skills
- Appreciate the role and relevance of physics in engineering and technology



## Course Objectives:

- The main objective of this course is to provide students with a comprehensive overview of the principles and practices of artificial intelligence (AI).
- The course will cover the following topics:
  - The history and foundations of AI, including its goals, challenges, and ethical issues.
  - The methods and techniques of AI, such as search, knowledge representation, reasoning, planning, learning, natural language processing, computer vision, and robotics.
  - The applications and limitations of AI, such as expert systems, game playing, speech recognition, face detection, self-driving cars, and social robots.
- The course will also help students to develop the following skills:
  - The ability to analyze and solve problems using AI techniques and tools.
  - The ability to design and implement AI systems and agents using programming languages and frameworks.
  - The ability to evaluate and compare the performance and effectiveness of different AI approaches and algorithms.
  - The ability to communicate and collaborate with others on AI-related projects and tasks.



### 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the samples, such as their identity, composition, structure, concentration, purity, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, signal processors, data analyzers, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as speed, cost, reliability, complexity, safety, etc.



# 2. To enable the students to understand about the analysis of chloride content, hardness, alkalinity of water.

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in water. Chloride ions are derived from various sources, such as natural salts, industrial effluents, sewage, and seawater intrusion. Chloride content is an important parameter for assessing the quality of water, as high levels of chloride can cause corrosion, taste and odor problems, and health issues.
- Hardness is the measure of the resistance of water to the formation of lather with soap. Hardness is caused by the presence of multivalent cations, such as calcium (Ca2+), magnesium (Mg2+), iron (Fe2+), and manganese (Mn2+), which form insoluble complexes with soap. Hardness can affect the performance of boilers, pipes, detergents, and domestic appliances, and can also cause scaling, staining, and health problems.
- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. Alkalinity is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonate (HCO3-), carbonate (CO32-), and hydroxide (OH-) ions, which act as buffers and maintain the pH of water. Alkalinity can affect the corrosion and precipitation of metals, the biological activity of aquatic organisms, and the disinfection efficiency of chlorine.

- The analysis of chloride content, hardness, and alkalinity of water is essential for determining the suitability of water for various purposes, such as drinking, irrigation, industrial, and recreational uses. The analysis can be done by using various methods, such as titration, colorimetry, spectrophotometry, ion chromatography, and electrochemical sensors. The analysis can also help in identifying the sources and causes of water pollution and contamination, and in designing appropriate treatment and remediation strategies.



# 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of a liquid.

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a liquid is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. pH = -log[H+]
- The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, lower than 7 being acidic, and higher than 7 being basic. Some common examples of liquids with different pH values are lemon juice (pH 2), vinegar (pH 3), water (pH 7), milk (pH 6.5), baking soda solution (pH 9), and ammonia solution (pH 11).
- To measure the pH of a liquid, one can use indicators, such as litmus paper, phenolphthalein, or universal indicator, that change color depending on the acidity or basicity of the liquid. Alternatively, one can use a pH meter, which is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode immersed in the liquid.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the force per unit length that must be applied to the surface to break it. Surface tension = F/L
- The surface tension of a liquid depends on the intermolecular forces between the molecules, the temperature, and the presence of any impurities or surfactants. Generally, liquids with stronger intermolecular forces have higher surface tension, and liquids with higher temperature have lower surface tension. Some common examples of liquids with different surface tension values are water (72 mN/m), ethanol (22 mN/m), mercury (486 mN/m), and soap solution (25 mN/m).
- To measure the surface tension of a liquid, one can use methods, such as the drop weight method, the capillary rise method, the Wilhelmy plate method, or the du Noüy ring method, that involve measuring the force or height of a liquid drop or column in contact with a solid or another liquid. Alternatively, one can use a tensiometer, which is an instrument that measures the surface tension by the force required to detach a ring or a plate from the liquid surface.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a liquid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a liquid. Viscosity = τ/γ
- The viscosity of a liquid depends on the intermolecular forces between the molecules, the temperature, and the pressure. Generally, liquids with stronger intermolecular forces have higher viscosity, and liquids with higher temperature or lower pressure have lower viscosity. Some common examples of liquids with different viscosity values are water (0.001 Pa.s), honey (10 Pa.s), glycerol (1.5 Pa.s), and motor oil (0.1 Pa.s).
- To measure the viscosity of a liquid, one can use methods, such as the falling ball method, the rotational viscometer method, the capillary viscometer method, or the oscillating piston method, that involve measuring the time, speed, torque, or pressure of a solid object moving through the liquid. Alternatively, one can use a rheometer, which is a device that measures the viscosity by applying a controlled shear stress or shear rate to the liquid and measuring the resulting deformation or flow.



# 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

Resins are organic compounds that are solid or semi-solid at room temperature and have a high molecular weight. They are usually derived from natural sources, such as plants, animals, or microorganisms, or synthesized from simple monomers, such as aldehydes, acids, or alcohols. Resins have various applications in industries, such as coatings, adhesives, plastics, rubber, and pharmaceuticals.

Some of the common types of resins and their preparation methods are:

- **Phenolic resins**: These are synthetic resins obtained by the condensation of phenol and formaldehyde in the presence of an acid or a base catalyst. Phenolic resins are thermosetting, meaning they cannot be remolded after curing. They have high mechanical strength, heat resistance, and chemical resistance. Phenolic resins are used for making laminates, plywood, molding compounds, and brake linings.

- **Polyester resins**: These are synthetic resins formed by the esterification of polyhydric alcohols and polybasic acids. Polyester resins are thermosetting or thermoplastic, depending on the degree of cross-linking. They have good mechanical properties, dimensional stability, and weather resistance. Polyester resins are used for making fiberglass, boat hulls, pipes, and electrical insulators.

- **Epoxy resins**: These are synthetic resins formed by the reaction of epoxides and amines, alcohols, or acids. Epoxy resins are thermosetting and have excellent adhesion, electrical insulation, and chemical resistance. Epoxy resins are used for making coatings, adhesives, composites, and electronic components.

- **Acrylic resins**: These are synthetic resins derived from acrylic or methacrylic acid or their esters. Acrylic resins are thermoplastic and have good transparency, color stability, and weather resistance. Acrylic resins are used for making paints, varnishes, plastics, and fibers.

- **Natural resins**: These are resins obtained from plants, animals, or microorganisms. Natural resins are usually complex mixtures of organic compounds, such as terpenes, phenols, and acids. Natural resins have various medicinal, cosmetic, and aromatic properties. Some examples of natural resins are:

  - **Rosin**: This is a resin obtained from the oleoresin of pine trees. Rosin is a brittle, yellowish solid that melts at about 100°C. Rosin is used for making varnishes, adhesives, soldering fluxes, and violin bows.

  - **Shellac**: This is a resin secreted by the lac insect on the branches of certain trees. Shellac is a hard, brittle, brownish solid that dissolves in alcohol. Shellac is used for making polishes, lacquers, and printing inks.

  - **Gum arabic**: This is a resin exuded by the acacia tree. Gum arabic is a white or yellowish powder that dissolves in water. Gum arabic is used for making paints, glues, candies, and cosmetics.

  - **Amber**: This is a fossilized resin from ancient coniferous trees. Amber is a hard, translucent, yellowish to brownish solid that can be polished. Amber is used for making jewelry, ornaments, and perfumes.



# 5. To enable the students to understand about the synthesis of organic compounds such as adipic acid and paracetamol by conventional and green route.

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is used as a raw material for the production of nylon-6,6, a synthetic polymer widely used in various applications.
- Paracetamol is an analgesic and antipyretic drug that is widely used to relieve pain and fever.
- Conventional and green routes of synthesis are two different approaches to produce organic compounds from different starting materials, using different reagents, catalysts, solvents, and reaction conditions.
- Conventional routes of synthesis are usually based on the use of fossil fuels, such as benzene, as the starting material, and involve the use of hazardous chemicals, such as nitric acid, as the oxidizing agent. These routes generate a lot of waste and emissions, such as nitrogen oxides, that are harmful to the environment and human health.
- Green routes of synthesis are based on the use of renewable and biodegradable resources, such as glucose, as the starting material, and involve the use of environmentally benign chemicals, such as hydrogen peroxide, as the oxidizing agent. These routes generate less waste and emissions, and are more energy-efficient and cost-effective.

## Synthesis of adipic acid by conventional and green route

- The conventional route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexane, cyclohexanol, or cyclohexanone, derived from benzene, with nitric acid in the presence of a metal catalyst, such as copper or vanadium. This route produces a mixture of adipic acid and other by-products, such as glutaric acid, succinic acid, and nitrous oxide. The adipic acid is then purified by crystallization, distillation, or extraction. This route requires high temperature and pressure, and generates a lot of waste and emissions, especially nitrous oxide, which is a potent greenhouse gas and an ozone-depleting substance.
- The green route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexene, derived from glucose, with hydrogen peroxide in the presence of a metal catalyst, such as tungsten or molybdenum. This route produces pure adipic acid in high yield, without any by-products. The adipic acid is then recovered by simple filtration or centrifugation. This route requires low temperature and pressure, and generates no waste and emissions, except water. This route is also known as the direct oxidation of cyclohexene, and was first reported by Behr et al. in 1998.

## Synthesis of paracetamol by conventional and green route

- The conventional route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of phenol, derived from benzene, with acetic anhydride in the presence of a catalyst, such as sulfuric acid or phosphoric acid. This route produces paracetamol and acetic acid as the main products, and some by-products, such as phenyl acetate and diacetyl. The paracetamol is then purified by crystallization, recrystallization, or chromatography. This route requires high temperature and pressure, and generates a lot of waste and emissions, especially sulfur dioxide and carbon dioxide.
- The green route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of 4-aminophenol, derived from glucose, with acetic acid in the presence of a catalyst, such as sodium acetate or sodium bicarbonate. This route produces paracetamol and water as the only products, without any by-products. The paracetamol is then recovered by simple filtration or centrifugation. This route requires low temperature and pressure, and generates no waste and emissions, except water. This route is also known as the one-pot synthesis of paracetamol, and was first reported by Reddy et al. in 2014.



Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with various tasks and topics. You have asked me to write on the topic of list of experiments. Here is my response:

# List of Experiments

An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or discover something new. Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables and measuring their effects on other variables. Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and execution. Some of the common types of experiments are:

- **Controlled experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher controls all the variables except one, which is manipulated to observe its effect on the outcome. The manipulated variable is called the independent variable, and the outcome variable is called the dependent variable. The other variables that are kept constant are called the control variables. Controlled experiments are often conducted in laboratories, where the conditions can be controlled and standardized. Controlled experiments are useful for establishing causal relationships between variables.

- **Natural experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher does not manipulate any variable, but observes the effects of a natural event or phenomenon on the outcome. The natural event or phenomenon acts as the independent variable, and the outcome variable is the dependent variable. The other variables that are not affected by the natural event or phenomenon are the control variables. Natural experiments are often conducted in the field, where the conditions are natural and realistic. Natural experiments are useful for studying the effects of rare or unpredictable events or phenomena.

- **Quasi-experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher manipulates one or more variables, but does not control all the other variables. The manipulated variables are the independent variables, and the outcome variable is the dependent variable. The other variables that are not controlled are called the confounding variables, as they may affect the outcome and confound the results. Quasi-experiments are often conducted in the field, where the conditions are natural and realistic, but not all the variables can be controlled. Quasi-experiments are useful for studying the effects of interventions or policies that cannot be randomly assigned or controlled.

- **Randomized experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher randomly assigns the participants or units to different groups or conditions. The groups or conditions are the independent variables, and the outcome variable is the dependent variable. The random assignment ensures that the groups or conditions are comparable and balanced on all the other variables, which are the control variables. Randomized experiments are often conducted in laboratories or in the field, where the conditions can be controlled and standardized. Randomized experiments are useful for eliminating confounding variables and establishing causal relationships between variables.

Some examples of experiments are:

- An experiment to test the effect of caffeine on memory. The researcher randomly assigns the participants to two groups: one group receives a cup of coffee, and the other group receives a cup of decaffeinated coffee. The independent variable is the type of coffee, and the dependent variable is the memory score. The researcher controls the other variables, such as the time of the day, the amount of coffee, the type of memory test, etc. The researcher then compares the memory scores of the two groups to see if there is a difference.

- An experiment to study the impact of a volcanic eruption on the climate. The researcher observes the changes in the temperature, precipitation, and cloud cover before and after the eruption. The independent variable is the volcanic eruption, and the dependent variables are the climate variables. The researcher does not control the other variables, such as the season, the location, the wind, etc. The researcher then analyzes the data to see if there is a correlation between the eruption and the climate.

- An experiment to evaluate the effectiveness of a tutoring program on academic performance. The researcher assigns the students to two groups: one group receives the tutoring program, and the other group does not. The independent variable is the tutoring program, and the dependent variable is the academic performance. The researcher does not control the other variables, such as the student's ability, motivation, background, etc. The researcher then compares the academic performance of the two groups to see if there is a difference.

- An experiment to compare the effects of different types of music on mood. The researcher randomly assigns the participants to four groups: one group listens to classical music, one group listens to rock music, one group listens to jazz music, and one group listens to no music. The independent variable is the type of music, and the dependent variable is the mood score. The researcher controls the other variables, such as the volume, the duration, the type of mood test, etc. The researcher then compares the mood scores of the four groups to see if there is a difference.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment.
- Calibration ensures that the instruments produce valid and reliable results that are consistent with the standards and specifications.
- Calibration is essential for maintaining the quality and safety of products, processes, and services that depend on accurate measurements.
- Calibration involves comparing the output of the instrument with a known reference value, such as a certified standard or a traceable reference material.
- Calibration also involves correcting any deviation or error in the instrument's performance, such as by adjusting the settings, replacing the components, or applying a correction factor.
- Calibration should be performed at regular intervals, depending on the frequency of use, the environmental conditions, the manufacturer's recommendations, and the in-house standard operating procedures .
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, including the date, the reference value, the measured value, the correction applied, the uncertainty, and the person responsible.
- Calibration should be verified by using check standards or quality control samples, which are materials with known or assigned values that are measured before and after the calibration.
- Calibration should be done by qualified and trained personnel, using appropriate and calibrated equipment and methods.



# 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method

Hardness of water is a measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water. These ions can form insoluble deposits in pipes, boilers, and other appliances that use water. Hardness of water can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA), which forms stable complexes with calcium and magnesium ions. The end point of the titration is detected by using an indicator that changes color when all the calcium and magnesium ions have been complexed by EDTA.

The procedure for determining the hardness of water by EDTA method is as follows   :

- Take a sample volume of 20 mL (V mL) of water in a clean Erlenmeyer flask.
- Dilute the sample to 40 mL by adding 20 mL of distilled water.
- Add 1 mL of ammonia buffer to adjust the pH to 10 ± 0.1. The buffer prevents the formation of insoluble hydroxides of calcium and magnesium.
- Add 1 or 2 drops of Eriochrome Black T indicator, which forms a wine-red complex with calcium and magnesium ions in the water sample.
- Titrate the sample with a standard solution of EDTA of known concentration (C M) from a burette, with constant stirring, until the color changes from wine-red to blue. This indicates that all the calcium and magnesium ions have been complexed by EDTA. Record the volume of EDTA used as V E mL.
- Repeat the titration with two more samples of water and take the average of the three readings of V E.

The hardness of water can be calculated by using the following formula  :

Hardness of water (mg/L as CaCO3) = (V E x C M x 1000) / V

where V E is the volume of EDTA used in mL, C M is the concentration of EDTA in mg CaCO3 per mL, and V is the volume of water sample in mL.

The hardness of water can be expressed in different units, such as grains per gallon (gpg), parts per million (ppm), or milligrams per liter (mg/L). One grain per gallon is equivalent to 17.1 ppm or 17.1 mg/L of hardness as CaCO3. The hardness of water can be classified as follows:

- Soft water: 0 - 60 mg/L as CaCO3
- Moderately hard water: 61 - 120 mg/L as CaCO3
- Hard water: 121 - 180 mg/L as CaCO3
- Very hard water: > 180 mg/L as CaCO3



# 3. Determination of Alkalinity of water sample

- Alkalinity is the measure of the ability of water to neutralize acids.
- Alkalinity is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides in water.
- Alkalinity is important for maintaining the pH and buffering capacity of water.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange.
- The titration can be done in two stages: the first stage is to titrate the water sample until the phenolphthalein indicator changes from pink to colorless, indicating the end point of the reaction between the acid and the hydroxides and half of the carbonates. The second stage is to continue the titration until the methyl orange indicator changes from yellow to orange, indicating the end point of the reaction between the acid and the remaining carbonates and bicarbonates.
- The alkalinity of the water sample can be calculated from the volume and concentration of the acid used and the volume of the water sample.
- The alkalinity can be expressed in different units, such as milligrams per liter (mg/L) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3), milliequivalents per liter (meq/L), or millimoles per liter (mmol/L).
- The alkalinity can be classified into different types, such as total alkalinity, phenolphthalein alkalinity, methyl orange alkalinity, carbonate alkalinity, bicarbonate alkalinity, and hydroxide alkalinity, depending on the species that contribute to the alkalinity and the indicator used in the titration.



# 4. Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a measured volume of a solution of unknown concentration (titrand).
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H+].
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a suitable indicator that changes color at a specific pH value (equivalence point) that corresponds to the stoichiometric point of the titration reaction.
- The indicator is added to the titrand solution and the titrant is added gradually from a burette until the color change occurs, indicating the end point of the titration.
- The volume of the titrant used is noted and the pH of the solution at the equivalence point is calculated using the titration equation and the known concentration of the titrant.
- The choice of the indicator depends on the type and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration. Some common indicators are phenolphthalein, methyl orange, bromothymol blue, and litmus.
- The titration curve is a plot of pH versus volume of titrant added, which shows the change in pH during the titration. The shape of the curve depends on the nature of the acid and base and the relative concentrations of the titrand and titrant.
- The titration curve can be used to determine the pH at any point of the titration, as well as the buffer capacity and the equivalence point of the titration.



Hello, I am Sydney, your AI assistant. I can help you with your studies. Here is some content on the topic of determination of surface tension of a given liquid.

# Determination of surface tension of a given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that causes them to behave as if they have a thin elastic skin on their surface.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the stalagmometer method.
- The capillary rise method is based on the principle that a liquid will rise or fall in a narrow tube (capillary) depending on the relative magnitude of the adhesive forces between the liquid and the tube, and the cohesive forces within the liquid.
- The drop weight method is based on the principle that the weight of a drop of liquid that detaches from a vertical capillary tube is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.
- The ring method is based on the principle that a horizontal ring of wire (du Noüy ring) immersed in a liquid and then pulled out will carry a film of liquid whose weight is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.
- The stalagmometer method is based on the principle that the number of drops of a liquid that fall from a capillary tube of known dimensions is inversely proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature and pressure of the fluid.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of the fluid depends on the pressure difference across the tube, the length and radius of the tube, and the viscosity of the fluid.
- The most common type of viscometer is the Ostwald viscometer, which consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a constant diameter capillary connecting them.
- The fluid is filled in the lower bulb and then sucked up to a higher level in the upper bulb using a suction pump or a rubber bulb.
- The fluid is then allowed to flow down through the capillary under the influence of gravity, and the time taken for the fluid to flow between two marked points on the tube is measured.
- The viscosity of the fluid can be calculated using the following formula:

$$\eta = \frac{\pi r^4 \rho g (t_1 - t_2)}{8 l (V_1 - V_2)}$$

where $\eta$ is the viscosity, $r$ is the radius of the capillary, $\rho$ is the density of the fluid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, $t_1$ and $t_2$ are the times taken for the fluid to flow between the two marked points, $l$ is the length of the capillary, and $V_1$ and $V_2$ are the volumes of the two bulbs.

- The viscosity of the fluid can also be expressed in terms of the relative viscosity, which is the ratio of the viscosity of the fluid to the viscosity of a reference fluid, usually water at the same temperature.
- The relative viscosity can be calculated using the following formula:

$$\frac{\eta}{\eta_0} = \frac{t}{t_0}$$

where $\eta_0$ and $t_0$ are the viscosity and the time taken for the reference fluid to flow through the viscometer, respectively.

- The relative viscosity can also be converted to the specific viscosity, which is the difference between the relative viscosity and 1, or the percentage viscosity, which is the specific viscosity multiplied by 100.
- The specific viscosity and the percentage viscosity can be calculated using the following formulas:

$$\eta_s = \frac{\eta}{\eta_0} - 1$$

$$\eta_p = 100 \eta_s$$

- The viscosity of a fluid can also be affected by the presence of solutes or impurities in the fluid, which can increase or decrease the intermolecular forces and the flow resistance of the fluid.
- The effect of solutes or impurities on the viscosity of a fluid can be quantified by the viscosity coefficient, which is the ratio of the percentage viscosity of the solution to the percentage concentration of the solute or impurity.
- The viscosity coefficient can be calculated using the following formula:

$$k = \frac{\eta_p}{c}$$

where $c$ is the percentage concentration of the solute or impurity.

- The viscosity coefficient can be used to compare the effect of different solutes or impurities on the viscosity of a fluid, or to determine the concentration of an unknown solute or impurity in a fluid by measuring its viscosity.



# 7. Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator.

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O.
- It is commonly used as a reducing agent in titrations involving oxidizing agents such as potassium permanganate (KMnO4) or potassium dichromate (K2Cr2O7).
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of an oxidizing agent, using an external indicator to detect the end point of the reaction.
- An external indicator is a substance that changes color when it comes in contact with the excess of the oxidizing agent, after the ferrous ammonium sulfate has been completely oxidized.
- One example of an external indicator is potassium ferricyanide (K3Fe(CN)6), which forms a blue precipitate of Prussian blue (Fe4[Fe(CN)6]3) with the excess of potassium permanganate, according to the reaction:

  2KMnO4 + 10K3Fe(CN)6 + 8H2O → 2MnO2 + 10K4Fe(CN)6 + 2KOH + 5Fe4[Fe(CN)6]3

- The procedure for the determination of the strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate by dissolving a known mass of KMnO4 crystals in distilled water and diluting to a known volume.
  - Transfer a known volume of the standard KMnO4 solution to a burette and note the initial reading.
  - Transfer a known volume of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution to a conical flask and add a few drops of dilute sulfuric acid to prevent the hydrolysis of iron(II) ions.
  - Add a few drops of potassium ferricyanide solution to a small porcelain dish and place it near the mouth of the conical flask.
  - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution with the standard KMnO4 solution, swirling the flask after each addition, until a faint blue color appears in the porcelain dish, indicating the end point of the titration.
  - Note the final reading of the burette and calculate the volume of KMnO4 solution used.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average volume of KMnO4 solution used.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution using the following formula:

    Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (Volume of KMnO4 × Normality of KMnO4 × Equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate) / (Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate)

    where the equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate is 196 g/mol and the normality of KMnO4 is the molarity multiplied by the number of electrons transferred per mole of KMnO4, which is 5 in this case.



### 8. Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator.

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is given by:

  `K2Cr2O7 + 6FeSO4 + 7H2SO4 -> Cr2(SO4)3 + 3K2SO4 + 6Fe2(SO4)3 + 7H2O`

- The end point of the titration can be detected by the change in colour of the solution from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of ferric ions.
- This colour change is the internal indicator of the titration, and no external indicator is required.
- The procedure for the determination of the strength of potassium dichromate is as follows:

  - Weigh accurately about 0.5 g of potassium dichromate and dissolve it in 100 mL of distilled water in a volumetric flask. This is the standard solution of potassium dichromate.
  - Pipette out 10 mL of the standard solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid.
  - Titrate the solution with ferrous ammonium sulphate solution of unknown strength from a burette, until the colour of the solution changes from green to reddish-brown.
  - Note down the burette reading and repeat the titration for concordant results.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution using the formula:

    `M1V1 = M2V2`

    where M1 and V1 are the molarity and volume of potassium dichromate solution, and M2 and V2 are the molarity and volume of ferrous ammonium sulphate solution.



# 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

- Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium, chlorine and oxygen with the formula Ca(OCl)Cl. It is used as a disinfectant and a bleaching agent.
- Available chlorine is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated from bleaching powder when it reacts with dilute acids. It is expressed as a percentage of the mass of bleaching powder.
- The reaction of bleaching powder with dilute acid is as follows:

  Ca(OCl)Cl + H2SO4 → CaSO4 + H2O + Cl2

- The available chlorine can be determined by titrating the chlorine gas evolved with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) using starch as an indicator. The reaction is as follows:

  Cl2 + 2Na2S2O3 → 2NaCl + Na2S4O6

- The procedure for the determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder is as follows:

  - Weigh about 0.5 g of bleaching powder and transfer it to a 250 mL conical flask.
  - Add about 100 mL of distilled water and shake well to dissolve the bleaching powder.
  - Add 10 mL of dilute sulfuric acid and stopper the flask immediately.
  - Allow the reaction to complete and the chlorine gas to escape.
  - Transfer the solution to a 250 mL volumetric flask and make up the volume with distilled water.
  - Pipette out 25 mL of the solution into a 250 mL conical flask and add 2 g of potassium iodide (KI).
  - Titrate the liberated iodine with 0.1 N sodium thiosulfate solution using starch as an indicator.
  - Note the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used at the end point.
  - Repeat the titration with a blank solution prepared by adding 10 mL of dilute sulfuric acid and 2 g of potassium iodide to 25 mL of distilled water.
  - Calculate the percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder sample using the following formula:

    % available chlorine = (B - S) x N x 35.46 x 100 / W

    where B is the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used for the blank titration, S is the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used for the sample titration, N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate solution, W is the weight of bleaching powder taken, and 35.46 is the equivalent weight of chlorine.



# 10. Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters. It originates from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage.
- Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. Therefore, it is important to monitor and control its concentration in drinking water and wastewater.
- The most common method for determining chloride content in water sample is the **Mohr method**. It is based on the precipitation of chloride as silver chloride (AgCl) by adding silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution in the presence of potassium chromate (K2CrO4) indicator.
- The procedure of the Mohr method is as follows:

  - Take a 100 mL aliquot of the water sample in a conical flask and add a few drops of potassium chromate indicator. The solution will turn yellow due to the formation of chromate ions (CrO4^2-^).
  - Titrate the solution with 0.02 N silver nitrate solution from a burette until a reddish-brown color appears. This indicates the end point of the titration, where all the chloride ions have been precipitated as silver chloride and the excess silver ions have reacted with the chromate ions to form silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).
  - Record the volume of silver nitrate solution used (V) and calculate the chloride content (C) in mg/L using the following formula:

    C = (V x N x 35.45 x 1000) / 100

    where N is the normality of silver nitrate solution and 35.45 is the equivalent weight of chloride ion.



# 11. Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The reaction ratio of phenol to formaldehyde determines the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks.
- The steps involved in the preparation of phenol formaldehyde resin are :

  - Weigh the appropriate quantity of the raw materials (phenol and formaldehyde) before mixing them in a reaction vessel.
  - Add the catalyst, which can be hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia, depending on the desired type of resin (acid-catalyzed or base-catalyzed).
  - Heat the mixture to initiate the polymerization reaction. The temperature and time of heating depend on the catalyst and the degree of polymerization required.
  - Monitor the viscosity, pH, and water content of the reaction mixture to control the quality and properties of the resin.
  - Stop the reaction when the desired molecular weight and degree of cross-linking are achieved. This can be done by adding water, neutralizing the catalyst, or cooling the mixture.
  - Filter and wash the resin to remove any impurities or unreacted materials.
  - Dry and cure the resin to complete the cross-linking process and improve the mechanical and thermal properties of the resin.

- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be used for various applications, such as adhesives, coatings, laminates, molding compounds, and wood products.



# 12. Preparation of Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is prepared by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst.
- The reaction involves three main steps: methylolation, condensation, and curing.
- Methylolation: In this step, formaldehyde reacts with urea to form monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea, which are the monomers of UF resin. The reaction is carried out in an aqueous solution with a formaldehyde to urea molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11 and a temperature of at least 80°C.
- Condensation: In this step, the monomers undergo further polymerization to form low molecular weight prepolymers, which are soluble and fusible. The reaction is catalyzed by an acid, such as sulfuric acid or oxalic acid, and the pH is lowered to 0.5-3.5. The degree of condensation depends on the reaction time, temperature, pH, and catalyst concentration.
- Curing: In this step, the prepolymers are cross-linked to form a three-dimensional network, which is insoluble and infusible. The curing is achieved by heating the resin with a hardener, such as ammonia or melamine, under pressure. The curing time and temperature depend on the resin composition, catalyst type, and hardener type.
- The properties of UF resin, such as viscosity, gel time, water resistance, and formaldehyde emission, can be controlled by varying the reaction conditions and additives.

## Adipic acid

- Adipic acid is the organic compound with the formula (CH2)4(COOH)2. It is the most important dicarboxylic acid, mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Adipic acid is produced from a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol called KA oil, the abbreviation of ketone-alcohol oil. The KA oil is oxidized with nitric acid to give adipic acid, via a multistep pathway .
- The steps involved in the oxidation of KA oil are :
  - Formation of nitrolic acid from nitric acid and KA oil
  - Formation of 6-nitroso-1,4-cyclohexanedione from nitrolic acid and KA oil
  - Formation of 6-nitro-1,4-cyclohexanedione from 6-nitroso-1,4-cyclohexanedione and nitric acid
  - Formation of adipic acid from 6-nitro-1,4-cyclohexanedione and water
- The overall reaction can be written as :

  C6H10O + 4 HNO3 -> C6H10O4 + 4 NO2 + 2 H2O

- Paracetamol is prepared from p-aminophenol by acetylating it with acetic anhydride in the presence of 3-4 drops of concentrated sulphuric acid as catalyst .
- The reaction is a nucleophilic acyl substitution, where the amine group of p-aminophenol attacks the carbonyl group of acetic anhydride, forming an amide bond and releasing acetic acid .

### 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a solution

- Cell conductance is the measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electric current through it.
- Cell conductance depends on the concentration, type and mobility of the ions present in the solution, as well as the temperature and geometry of the cell.
- Cell conductance is measured by applying an alternating current (AC) of known frequency and amplitude to the cell and measuring the resulting voltage drop across the cell.
- Cell conductance is related to the cell resistance by the equation: `G = 1/R`, where `G` is the cell conductance, `R` is the cell resistance, and `1` is the unit of conductance (siemens or S).
- Cell conductance is also related to the specific conductance or conductivity of the solution by the equation: `G = k x A / l`, where `k` is the specific conductance, `A` is the cross-sectional area of the cell, and `l` is the length of the cell.
- To determine the cell conductance of a solution, the following steps are followed:

  - Prepare a standard solution of known specific conductance, such as potassium chloride (KCl).
  - Rinse the cell with distilled water and then with the standard solution.
  - Fill the cell with the standard solution and connect it to the conductance meter.
  - Adjust the frequency and amplitude of the AC source to obtain a stable reading on the meter.
  - Record the cell resistance and calculate the cell constant using the equation: `C = k x R`, where `C` is the cell constant and `k` is the specific conductance of the standard solution.
  - Rinse the cell with distilled water and then with the unknown solution.
  - Fill the cell with the unknown solution and connect it to the conductance meter.
  - Adjust the frequency and amplitude of the AC source to obtain a stable reading on the meter.
  - Record the cell resistance and calculate the cell conductance using the equation: `G = 1/R`.
  - Repeat the procedure for different concentrations of the unknown solution and plot a graph of cell conductance versus concentration.
  - Analyze the graph to determine the relationship between cell conductance and concentration for the unknown solution.



# 15. Determination of rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Hydrolysis of esters is the reaction of esters with water to produce carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- Hydrolysis of esters is catalyzed by either an acid or a base. Acidic hydrolysis is the reverse of esterification, while basic hydrolysis is also known as saponification.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on the nature of the ester, the concentration of the catalyst, the temperature, and the solvent.
- The rate law for the hydrolysis of esters can be written as:

  `rate = k[ester][H2O][H+]` for acidic hydrolysis

  `rate = k[ester][OH-]` for basic hydrolysis

  where k is the rate constant, and [ ] denotes the concentration of the species.
- The rate constant k can be determined experimentally by measuring the rate of the reaction under different conditions, such as varying the concentration of the catalyst, the ester, or the water, or changing the temperature or the solvent.
- One method to determine the rate constant of acid-catalyzed hydrolysis of an ester is based on the following steps:

  - Prepare a solution of the ester and a large excess of water containing a known concentration of a strong acid, such as hydrochloric acid, in a conical flask.
  - Heat the flask in a water bath at a constant temperature and shake it occasionally.
  - At regular intervals of time, withdraw a small sample of the reaction mixture and titrate it against a standard solution of sodium hydroxide, using phenolphthalein as an indicator.
  - Record the volume of sodium hydroxide required to reach the end point of the titration, and the corresponding time elapsed since the start of the reaction.
  - Repeat the procedure until the volume of sodium hydroxide becomes constant, indicating that the reaction has reached equilibrium.
  - Plot a graph of log((V∞ - V0) / (V∞ - Vt)) versus t, where V∞ is the final volume of sodium hydroxide at equilibrium, V0 is the initial volume of sodium hydroxide at the start of the reaction, and Vt is the volume of sodium hydroxide at time t.
  - The slope of the graph is equal to 2.303/k, where k is the rate constant of the reaction.
  - Calculate the value of k from the slope of the graph.



# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Element detection is the process of determining the presence or absence of certain elements in an organic compound, such as carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, halogens, etc.
- Identification of functional groups is the process of determining the type and location of the specific groups of atoms that are responsible for the characteristic chemical reactions of an organic compound, such as alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, amines, etc.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups are important for the qualitative analysis of organic compounds, which helps to understand their structure, properties, and interactions.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups can be done by various physical and chemical tests, such as solubility, color, odor, melting point, boiling point, flame test, precipitation test, etc.
- Some of the common tests for element detection and identification of functional groups are:

  - Carbon and hydrogen: These elements are present in all organic compounds. They can be detected by heating the compound with copper oxide in a test tube. The carbon is oxidized to carbon dioxide, which turns lime water milky, and the hydrogen is oxidized to water, which condenses on the cooler part of the test tube.
  - Oxygen: This element is present in most organic compounds, except hydrocarbons. It can be detected by heating the compound with sodium metal in a test tube. The oxygen is reduced to sodium oxide, which dissolves in water to form sodium hydroxide, which turns red litmus paper blue.
  - Nitrogen: This element is present in many organic compounds, such as amines, amides, nitriles, etc. It can be detected by heating the compound with sodium metal in a test tube. The nitrogen is reduced to ammonia, which can be recognized by its pungent smell and its ability to turn red litmus paper blue and moist turmeric paper brown.
  - Sulfur: This element is present in some organic compounds, such as thiols, sulfides, sulfones, etc. It can be detected by heating the compound with sodium metal in a test tube. The sulfur is reduced to hydrogen sulfide, which can be recognized by its rotten egg smell and its ability to turn lead acetate paper black.
  - Halogens: These elements are present in some organic compounds, such as alkyl halides, aryl halides, etc. They can be detected by heating the compound with sodium metal in a test tube. The halogens are reduced to sodium halides, which can be extracted with water and tested with silver nitrate solution. The silver halides formed have different colors and solubilities, which can be used to identify the halogen. For example, silver chloride is white and soluble in ammonia, silver bromide is pale yellow and slightly soluble in ammonia, and silver iodide is yellow and insoluble in ammonia.
  - Alcoholic –OH group: This functional group is present in organic compounds that have one or more hydroxyl groups attached to a carbon atom, such as alcohols, phenols, etc. It can be detected by the following tests:
    - Lucas test: This test is used to distinguish between primary, secondary, and tertiary alcohols. The alcohol is mixed with Lucas reagent, which is a solution of zinc chloride and concentrated hydrochloric acid. The alcohol reacts with the reagent to form an alkyl chloride, which is insoluble in the aqueous layer and forms a turbid or cloudy solution. The rate of reaction depends on the type of alcohol. Tertiary alcohols react immediately, secondary alcohols react within 5 minutes, and primary alcohols do not react at room temperature.
    - Esterification test: This test is used to confirm the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The alcohol is mixed with an acid anhydride, such as acetic anhydride, and a few drops of concentrated sulfuric acid as a catalyst. The alcohol reacts with the anhydride to form an ester, which has a fruity smell. The ester can be extracted with ether and tested with sodium hydroxide solution. The ester is hydrolyzed to form a salt of the carboxylic acid and the alcohol, which turns red litmus paper blue.
  - Carbonyls (Aldehydes and Ketones): These functional groups are present in organic compounds that have a carbon atom double bonded to an oxygen atom, such as aldehydes, ketones, etc. They can be detected by the

## Course Outcomes:

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students are expected to know and be able to do by the end of a course.
- Course outcomes are aligned with the course objectives, which are derived from the program outcomes and the institutional mission and vision.
- Course outcomes are measurable, observable, and achievable within the duration and scope of the course.
- Course outcomes are written in terms of student learning, using verbs that indicate the level of cognitive skills required, such as analyze, apply, create, evaluate, etc.
- Course outcomes are communicated to the students at the beginning of the course and throughout the course delivery, using various methods such as syllabus, rubrics, feedback, etc.
- Course outcomes are assessed using appropriate tools and methods, such as exams, assignments, projects, portfolios, etc.
- Course outcomes are used to evaluate the effectiveness of the course design, delivery, and improvement, as well as the student achievement and satisfaction.

# Course Outcomes Bloom's Taxonomy

- Course outcomes are brief statements that describe what students will be expected to learn by the end of the course.
- Course outcomes should be aligned with the course content, activities, and assessments, and should reflect the level of cognitive skills that students need to demonstrate.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a model of cognitive skills that can be used to classify educational learning objectives and to design course outcomes that support student learning at multiple levels of cognition .
- Bloom's taxonomy consists of six levels of cognitive skills, from lower-order to higher-order: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create .
- Remembering involves recalling facts, terms, concepts, or procedures from memory .
- Understanding involves explaining, interpreting, summarizing, or paraphrasing the meaning of information .
- Applying involves using knowledge or skills to solve problems or perform tasks in new situations .
- Analyzing involves breaking down information into parts, identifying relationships, or examining causes and effects .
- Evaluating involves judging the quality, value, or relevance of information, arguments, or solutions based on criteria or standards .
- Creating involves generating, designing, planning, or producing new or original ideas, products, or solutions .
- Each level of Bloom's taxonomy requires a different type of verb to describe the learning outcome. For example, verbs for remembering include: define, list, name, recall, recognize, etc. Verbs for creating include: compose, construct, design, develop, invent, etc .
- Course outcomes should use verbs that match the level of cognitive skills that students are expected to achieve by the end of the course. For example, a course outcome that uses the verb "describe" implies that students will only need to remember or understand the information, while a course outcome that uses the verb "analyze" implies that students will need to apply or analyze the information .
- Course outcomes should also specify the content or context of the learning outcome, such as the topic, concept, skill, or situation that students will need to demonstrate their learning. For example, a course outcome that uses the verb "apply" should also indicate what knowledge or skill students will apply, and to what problem or task .
- Course outcomes should be measurable, observable, and achievable, meaning that they can be assessed using appropriate methods and tools, and that they are realistic and attainable for the students and the course .
- Course outcomes should be written from the perspective of the students, using phrases such as "students will be able to" or "by the end of the course, students will" to indicate the expected learning outcomes .
- Course outcomes should be reviewed and revised periodically to ensure that they are aligned with the course content, activities, and assessments, and that they reflect the current expectations and standards of the discipline and the institution .



# Level

- A level is a tool or device that is used to measure or indicate whether a surface is horizontal (level) or vertical (plumb).
- A level consists of a horizontal tube or vial that contains a liquid (usually alcohol or water) and an air bubble. The position of the bubble indicates the alignment of the surface relative to the earth's gravity.
- A level can be used for various purposes, such as construction, carpentry, engineering, surveying, landscaping, photography, etc.
- There are different types of levels, such as spirit levels, laser levels, digital levels, water levels, etc. Each type has its own advantages and disadvantages depending on the application and accuracy required.
- Some common features of levels are:

  - Length: The length of the level determines the size of the surface that can be measured or aligned. Longer levels are more accurate but less portable and convenient.
  - Vials: The number and orientation of the vials on the level affect the versatility and functionality of the tool. Some levels have only one horizontal vial, while others have multiple vials at different angles (such as 45°, 90°, etc.).
  - Sensitivity: The sensitivity of the level refers to how much the bubble moves when the surface is slightly tilted. Higher sensitivity means more precision but also more difficulty in reading the bubble.
  - Material: The material of the level affects the durability, weight, and appearance of the tool. Common materials are wood, metal, plastic, or composite.
  - Features: Some levels have additional features that enhance their usability, such as magnets, handles, lights, digital displays, etc.

### CO-1 Get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments. K3

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Different analytical instruments have different applications, advantages, and limitations depending on the type of sample, the property of interest, and the level of accuracy and precision required.
- Some examples of analytical instruments are:

  - Spectrophotometers: These instruments measure the amount of light absorbed or emitted by a sample at different wavelengths. They can be used to determine the concentration, structure, or identity of substances in a sample based on their characteristic absorption or emission spectra.
  - Chromatographs: These instruments separate the components of a mixture based on their different affinities for a stationary phase (such as a column or a paper) and a mobile phase (such as a solvent or a gas). They can be used to identify, quantify, or purify the components of a mixture based on their retention times or elution volumes.
  - Mass spectrometers: These instruments measure the mass-to-charge ratio of ions generated from a sample by various ionization methods. They can be used to determine the molecular weight, structure, or composition of substances in a sample based on their characteristic mass spectra.
  - Electrochemical instruments: These instruments measure the electrical potential, current, or charge of a sample or an electrode in an electrochemical cell. They can be used to determine the concentration, activity, or redox state of substances in a sample based on their characteristic electrochemical behavior.
  - Microscopes: These instruments magnify the image of a sample using visible light, electrons, or other forms of radiation. They can be used to observe the morphology, structure, or function of cells, tissues, or materials in a sample based on their characteristic optical or electron properties.



# CO2: Molecular and System Properties

Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a colorless and odorless gas that is relatively nontoxic and noncombustible, but it is heavier than air and may asphyxiate by the displacement of air. When CO2 is dissolved in water, it forms a weak acid called carbonic acid (H2CO3). CO2 has a molecular weight of 44.0095 g/mol and a linear molecular geometry with a bond angle of 180 degrees. CO2 has two polar C=O bonds, but the molecule is nonpolar because the bond dipoles cancel each other out.

Surface tension is the energy required to increase the surface area of a liquid by a given amount. It is a measure of the cohesive forces between the molecules in the liquid. The stronger the intermolecular interactions, the greater the surface tension. CO2 has weak intermolecular forces, mainly London dispersion forces, which result in a low surface tension. However, the surface tension of CO2 can be affected by the presence of other substances, such as water or amines, that can form hydrogen bonds or other interactions with CO2 molecules .

Some of the factors that influence the surface tension of CO2-loaded solutions are:

- The concentration of CO2: The higher the CO2 loading, the lower the surface tension, because CO2 molecules reduce the cohesive forces between the solvent molecules .
- The temperature: The higher the temperature, the lower the surface tension, because thermal energy increases the molecular motion and weakens the intermolecular forces .
- The type and concentration of the solvent: Different solvents have different intermolecular forces and polarities, which affect the interactions with CO2 molecules. For example, water has a high surface tension due to its strong hydrogen bonding, but it decreases when CO2 is dissolved in it. Amines, such as monoethanolamine (MEA), have lower surface tensions than water, but they increase when CO2 is dissolved in them, because they form complexes with CO2 that increase the cohesive forces .

The surface tension of CO2 and CO2-loaded solutions can be measured by various methods, such as the drop weight method, the pendant drop method, the Wilhelmy plate method, or the maximum bubble pressure method . These methods involve creating a drop or a bubble of the liquid and measuring the force or pressure required to deform or detach it from the surface. The surface tension can be calculated from the force or pressure and the geometry of the drop or bubble.



# Viscosity, Conductance of Solution, Chloride and Iron Content in the Water

## Viscosity
- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to deformation under shear stress.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature, pressure, and composition of the fluid.
- Viscosity of water decreases with increasing temperature and decreases with increasing salinity.
- Viscosity of water at 20°C is 1.002 mPa s.
- Viscosity of water at 100°C is 0.282 mPa s.
- Viscosity of seawater at 20°C is 1.08 mPa s.

## Conductance of Solution
- Conductance of solution is a measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electric current.
- Conductance of solution depends on the concentration, type, and mobility of the ions in the solution, as well as the temperature and pressure of the solution.
- Conductance of solution increases with increasing concentration and temperature of the solution.
- Conductance of solution is inversely proportional to the viscosity of the solution.
- Conductance of solution is measured in Siemens per meter (S/m) or microsiemens per centimeter (µS/cm).
- Conductance of pure water at 25°C is about 0.05 µS/cm.
- Conductance of drinking water at 25°C is in the range of 200–800 µS/cm.
- Conductance of seawater at 25°C is about 50 mS/cm (or 0.05 S/cm).

## Chloride and Iron Content in the Water
- Chloride and iron are two common dissolved solids in water that affect its quality and properties.
- Chloride is a negatively charged ion (Cl-) that comes from natural sources such as seawater, rocks, and volcanic activity, or from human activities such as agriculture, industry, and wastewater.
- Chloride increases the salinity, conductivity, and corrosivity of water, and can affect the taste and odor of water.
- Chloride can also interfere with the disinfection process and cause the formation of harmful byproducts such as chloramines and trihalomethanes.
- Chloride concentration in water is measured in milligrams per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm).
- Chloride concentration in freshwater is usually less than 250 mg/L, while in seawater it is about 19,000 mg/L.
- Chloride concentration in drinking water is regulated by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to be less than 250 mg/L for aesthetic reasons.
- Iron is a positively charged ion (Fe2+ or Fe3+) that comes from natural sources such as soil, rocks, and groundwater, or from human activities such as mining, industry, and corrosion of pipes and fittings.
- Iron increases the hardness, color, and turbidity of water, and can cause staining of plumbing fixtures, laundry, and dishes.
- Iron can also affect the taste and odor of water, and promote the growth of iron bacteria that can clog pipes and filters.
- Iron concentration in water is measured in milligrams per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm).
- Iron concentration in freshwater is usually less than 0.3 mg/L, while in seawater it is about 0.01 mg/L.
- Iron concentration in drinking water is regulated by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to be less than 0.3 mg/L for aesthetic reasons.

# K3

- K3 is a type of **algebraic K-theory**, which is a branch of mathematics that studies the abstract properties of objects and maps using **functors** and **exact sequences**.
- Algebraic K-theory is based on the idea of **Grothendieck groups**, which are constructed by taking the **free abelian group** generated by the isomorphism classes of objects in a given category, and then **quotienting** by the subgroup generated by the relations of the form [A] - [B] + [C], where A, B, and C are objects in a **short exact sequence**.
- K3 is the third functor in the **Quillen–Lichtenbaum spectral sequence**, which relates the algebraic K-theory of a **ring** or a **scheme** to its **etale cohomology**. Etale cohomology is a generalization of the classical **Galois cohomology** that uses **etale morphisms** instead of field extensions.
- K3 has many applications and connections to other areas of mathematics, such as **number theory**, **algebraic geometry**, **topology**, and **representation theory**. For example, K3 can be used to study the **Brauer group** of a scheme, which is a group that classifies the **central simple algebras** over the scheme. K3 can also be used to study the **Chow group** of a scheme, which is a group that classifies the **algebraic cycles** on the scheme modulo **rational equivalence**.
- K3 is also the name of a special class of **complex surfaces**, which are two-dimensional complex manifolds that have **trivial canonical bundle** and **no global holomorphic vector fields**. These surfaces have many remarkable properties, such as having **Picard number** at most 20, having **transcendental Hodge numbers** (2,2), and being **simply connected**. K3 surfaces are named after the **Kummer surface**, the **Kahler condition**, and the **Kodaira dimension**.



# CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness and alkalinity are two important parameters of water quality that indicate the concentration of dissolved minerals and bases in water respectively.
- Hardness is caused by compounds of calcium and magnesium, and by a variety of other metals, such as iron, manganese, and zinc. Hard water can cause scaling, corrosion, and reduced efficiency of water appliances and equipment.
- Alkalinity measures the total amount of bases present in water that can neutralize acids. Alkalinity is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions in water. Alkalinity can affect the pH, buffering capacity, and biological activity of water.
- Hardness and alkalinity are related through common ions formed in aquatic systems. Specifically, the counter-ions associated with the bicarbonate and carbonate fraction of alkalinity are the principal cations responsible for hardness (usually Ca ++ and Mg ++) (Equations 3 and 4).
- Hardness and alkalinity can be measured by titration methods using standard solutions of acids and indicators. The following steps are involved in the measurement of hardness and alkalinity of water:
  - Collect a water sample in a clean container and measure its volume.
  - Add a few drops of an indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange, to the water sample and observe the color change.
  - If the water sample turns pink with phenolphthalein, it indicates the presence of carbonate and hydroxide alkalinity. Titrate the water sample with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) until the pink color disappears. Record the volume of acid used. This is the phenolphthalein alkalinity (P-alkalinity).
  - If the water sample turns yellow with methyl orange, it indicates the presence of bicarbonate alkalinity. Titrate the water sample with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) until the yellow color turns orange. Record the volume of acid used. This is the total alkalinity (T-alkalinity).
  - The carbonate alkalinity (C-alkalinity) can be calculated by subtracting the P-alkalinity from the T-alkalinity. The hydroxide alkalinity (OH-alkalinity) can be calculated by subtracting the C-alkalinity from the P-alkalinity.
  - To measure the hardness of water, add a few drops of an indicator, such as eriochrome black T (EBT), to the water sample and observe the color change. If the water sample turns wine red with EBT, it indicates the presence of calcium and magnesium ions. Titrate the water sample with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) until the wine red color turns blue. Record the volume of EDTA used. This is the total hardness (TH) of water.
  - The calcium hardness (CaH) and magnesium hardness (MgH) can be calculated by using separate indicators, such as murexide and calmagite, and titrating with EDTA. Alternatively, the calcium hardness can be calculated by subtracting the magnesium hardness from the total hardness.
  - The hardness and alkalinity of water can be expressed in different units, such as milligrams per liter (mg/L), parts per million (ppm), or grains per gallon (gpg) as calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalents. To convert from mg/L to ppm, multiply by 1. To convert from mg/L to gpg, divide by 17.1. To convert from ppm to gpg, divide by 17.1. To convert from gpg to mg/L, multiply by 17.1. To convert from gpg to ppm, multiply by 17.1.



# CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound that has a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. Phenol is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol has many applications in industry, medicine, and organic synthesis.

There are several methods to prepare phenol from different starting materials. Some of the common methods are:

- **From haloarenes:** Haloarenes are aromatic compounds that have one or more halogen atoms (-X) attached to a benzene ring. Examples of haloarenes are chlorobenzene, bromobenzene, and iodobenzene. Haloarenes can be converted to phenol by heating them with sodium hydroxide (NaOH) at high temperature and pressure. This is called the Dow process. The reaction involves the replacement of the halogen atom by a hydroxyl group. For example, chlorobenzene reacts with NaOH to form sodium phenoxide (NaOC6H5), which is then acidified with dilute acid (H+) to give phenol.

  Dow process

- **From benzene:** Benzene is the simplest aromatic compound that has a six-carbon ring with alternating single and double bonds. Benzene can be converted to phenol by two main methods:

  - **By sulphonation and hydrolysis:** Benzene reacts with concentrated sulfuric acid (H2SO4) to form benzene sulphonic acid (C6H5SO3H), which is then fused with sodium hydroxide (NaOH) at high temperature to form sodium phenoxide (NaOC6H5), which is then acidified with dilute acid (H+) to give phenol. This is called the Raschig process.

    Raschig process

  - **By oxidation of cumene:** Cumene is an alkylbenzene that has an isopropyl group (-CH(CH3)2) attached to a benzene ring. Cumene can be oxidized by air (O2) in the presence of a catalyst to form cumene hydroperoxide (C6H5C(CH3)2OOH), which is then decomposed by heating with dilute acid (H+) to form phenol and acetone (CH3COCH3). This is called the cumene process.

    Cumene process

- **From diazonium salts:** Diazonium salts are organic compounds that have a nitrogen-nitrogen double bond (-N=N-) attached to an aromatic ring. Examples of diazonium salts are benzenediazonium chloride (C6H5N2Cl) and anisole diazonium chloride (C6H5OCH3N2Cl). Diazonium salts can be converted to phenol by heating them with water (H2O) or by treating them with dilute acid (H+) or alkaline solution (OH-). This is called the Sandmeyer reaction. The reaction involves the replacement of the diazonium group by a hydroxyl group. For example, benzenediazonium chloride reacts with water to form phenol and nitrogen gas (N2).

  Sandmeyer reaction

These are some of the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol. There are other methods to synthesize phenol from different sources, such as coal tar, phenylboronic acid, and phenylmagnesium bromide. However, these methods are less common or less efficient than the ones mentioned above.



# Formaldehyde & Urea Formaldehyde Resin, Adipic Acid and Paracetamol

- Formaldehyde is a colorless, flammable, strong-smelling chemical that is used in building materials and to produce many household products.
- Formaldehyde can cause irritation of the eyes, nose, and throat, and can increase the risk of some cancers.
- Urea formaldehyde (UF) is a nontransparent thermosetting resin or polymer that is produced from urea and formaldehyde.
- UF is used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF can release formaldehyde gas into the air, which can cause health problems for people who are exposed to it.
- UF can also show the appearance of crystal domains, which can affect its morphology and crystallinity .
- Adipic acid is a white, crystalline organic acid that is mainly used to produce nylon and polyurethane.
- Adipic acid can also be used as a modifier to reduce the free formaldehyde content in UF resin.
- Adipic acid can react with formaldehyde and urea to form a cross-linked network that can improve the stability and performance of UF resin.
- Paracetamol is a common painkiller and antipyretic (fever reducer) that is widely used in medicine.
- Paracetamol is usually safe and effective when taken as directed, but it can cause liver damage or failure if taken in large doses or with alcohol.
- Paracetamol is synthesized from phenol and nitric acid, followed by reduction and acetylation.
- Paracetamol works by inhibiting the enzyme cyclooxygenase (COX), which is involved in the production of prostaglandins, which are inflammatory mediators.

# K3

- K3 is a synthetic, artificially produced form of vitamin K that doesn’t occur naturally .
- K3 is also known as menadione .
- K3 may be converted into K2 in your liver.
- K3 is important for blood clotting and bone health.
- K3 has demonstrated anticancer and antibacterial properties in test-tube studies.
- K3 is not approved for human use by the FDA and may cause serious side effects such as liver damage, anemia, and oxidative stress .
- K3 is sometimes used as a supplement for animals, especially poultry and fish, to prevent bleeding disorders .
- K3 is also the name of a schedule form for reporting a partner’s share of income, deductions, credits, etc. from international sources for U.S. tax purposes.

### CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction. K3

- The rate constant of a reaction is a proportionality factor that relates the reaction rate to the concentrations of the reactants.
- The rate constant depends on the temperature, the presence of a catalyst, and the nature of the reaction.
- The rate constant can have different units depending on the order of the reaction.
- For a termolecular reaction, where three molecules collide simultaneously to form products, the reaction rate is described by r=k3[A][B][C], where k3 is a termolecular rate constant.
- Termolecular reactions are rare because the probability of three molecules colliding at the same time is very low.
- To estimate the rate constant of a termolecular reaction, one can use the following methods :
  - Experimental measurement: This involves measuring the concentrations of the reactants and the products at different times and plotting the data to obtain the rate law and the rate constant.
  - Arrhenius equation: This relates the rate constant to the temperature and the activation energy of the reaction. The equation is k=Ae^(-Ea/RT), where A is the pre-exponential factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.
  - Collision theory: This assumes that the rate of a reaction is proportional to the frequency and the effectiveness of the collisions between the molecules. The equation is k=ZABe^(-Ea/RT), where ZAB is the collision frequency factor, and the other terms are the same as in the Arrhenius equation.
