# Page 31 of 40

- The topic of this page is **the structure and function of the human eye**.
- The human eye is a complex organ that allows us to see the world around us. It consists of several parts, each with a specific role in vision.
- The main parts of the human eye are:

  - The **cornea**: a transparent layer that covers the front of the eye and refracts (bends) light rays entering the eye.
  - The **iris**: a circular muscle that controls the size of the pupil, the opening in the center of the iris that regulates the amount of light entering the eye.
  - The **lens**: a flexible structure behind the pupil that changes shape to focus light rays onto the retina, the light-sensitive layer at the back of the eye.
  - The **ciliary body**: a ring of muscles that attaches to the lens and helps adjust its shape and position.
  - The **aqueous humor**: a clear fluid that fills the space between the cornea and the lens and provides nourishment and pressure to the eye.
  - The **vitreous humor**: a gel-like substance that fills the space between the lens and the retina and helps maintain the shape of the eye.
  - The **retina**: a thin layer of cells that lines the inner wall of the eye and converts light signals into electrical impulses that are sent to the brain via the optic nerve.
  - The **macula**: a small area in the center of the retina that contains a high concentration of cone cells, which are responsible for color vision and sharp central vision.
  - The **fovea**: a tiny pit in the center of the macula that contains only cone cells and provides the highest visual acuity (resolution).
  - The **optic nerve**: a bundle of nerve fibers that carries the electrical impulses from the retina to the brain, where they are processed and interpreted as images.
  - The **optic disc**: a circular area where the optic nerve exits the eye and where there are no photoreceptor cells, creating a blind spot in the visual field.
  - The **sclera**: a tough white layer that covers the outer surface of the eye and protects it from injury and infection.
  - The **conjunctiva**: a thin membrane that covers the sclera and the inner surface of the eyelids and lubricates the eye with tears.
  - The **eyelids**: movable folds of skin that cover the eye and protect it from dust, debris, and excessive light.
  - The **eyelashes**: short hairs that grow on the edges of the eyelids and help prevent foreign particles from entering the eye.
  - The **eyebrows**: curved rows of hairs above the eyes that help prevent sweat and rain from dripping into the eye.
  - The **tear glands**: small glands located at the corners of the eye that produce tears, which moisten, cleanse, and protect the eye from infection.
  - The **tear ducts**: small tubes that drain the excess tears from the eye to the nose.

- The human eye works by:

  - Receiving light rays from the environment and refracting them through the cornea, the pupil, and the lens to form an inverted image on the retina.
  - Converting the light signals into electrical impulses by the photoreceptor cells (rods and cones) in the retina. Rods are more sensitive to dim light and provide black-and-white vision, while cones are more sensitive to bright light and provide color vision.
  - Transmitting the electrical impulses from the retina to the brain via the optic nerve, where they are processed and interpreted as images by the visual cortex in the occipital lobe.
  - Adjusting the focus, brightness, and color of the image by changing the shape and position of the lens, the size of the pupil, and the activity of the cone cells, respectively.
  - Coordinating the movement and alignment of the eyes by the extraocular muscles, which are controlled by the cranial nerves and the brainstem. This allows the eyes to track objects and produce binocular vision, which is the ability to perceive depth and distance by combining the images from both eyes.

# ENGINEERING MATHEMATICS LAB KCS

- Engineering Mathematics Lab KCS is a course that aims to provide students with the practical skills and knowledge of applying mathematical concepts and methods to solve engineering problems.
- The course consists of laboratory sessions, where students work on various experiments and projects using software tools such as MATLAB, Mathematica, and Maple.
- The course covers topics such as linear algebra, differential equations, numerical methods, optimization, statistics, and discrete mathematics.
- The course objectives are to:
  - Enhance the students' understanding of the theoretical aspects of engineering mathematics and their applications in engineering disciplines.
  - Develop the students' ability to use software tools for mathematical modeling, simulation, analysis, and visualization of engineering problems.
  - Improve the students' communication and presentation skills by preparing and submitting lab reports and giving oral presentations.
  - Encourage the students' creativity and innovation by designing and implementing their own projects based on engineering mathematics.
- The course outcomes are to:
  - Apply mathematical concepts and methods to model and solve engineering problems using software tools.
  - Analyze and interpret the results of mathematical computations and simulations using software tools.
  - Communicate and present the mathematical solutions and findings effectively using appropriate formats and styles.
  - Demonstrate the skills of independent learning, teamwork, and critical thinking in engineering mathematics.



# Course Objectives:

- The course objectives are the specific learning outcomes that the students are expected to achieve by the end of the course.
- The course objectives should be aligned with the course description, the course learning outcomes, and the assessment methods.
- The course objectives should be clear, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound.
- The course objectives should be written using action verbs that indicate the level of cognitive skills required, such as recall, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, or create.
- The course objectives should be communicated to the students at the beginning of the course and throughout the course as needed.
- The course objectives should be reviewed and revised periodically to ensure that they reflect the current needs and expectations of the students and the discipline.



# 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles, applications, or techniques, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the samples, such as their identity, composition, structure, concentration, purity, activity, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, amplifiers, filters, displays, etc., that work together to generate, manipulate, and process signals from the samples.
- Analytical instruments can have different characteristics, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, resolution, range, speed, etc., that affect their performance and reliability.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as cost, availability, ease of use, maintenance, safety, etc., that influence their suitability and feasibility for different applications.



# Analysis of Chloride Content, Hardness, and Alkalinity

- Chloride content is a measure of the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in a water sample. Chloride ions are derived from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage. Chloride content affects the taste, corrosion, and disinfection of water.
- Hardness is a measure of the amount of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions present in a water sample. These ions are derived from the dissolution of minerals, such as limestone, dolomite, and gypsum. Hardness affects the lathering, scaling, and softening of water.
- Alkalinity is a measure of the capacity of a water sample to neutralize acids. It is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonate (HCO3-), carbonate (CO32-), and hydroxide (OH-) ions. Alkalinity affects the pH, buffering, and coagulation of water.

## Methods of Analysis

- Chloride content can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of silver nitrate (AgNO3) using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point is marked by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).
- Hardness can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator. The end point is marked by a color change from wine red to blue.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The end point is marked by a color change from pink to colorless (phenolphthalein) and from yellow to orange (methyl orange).

## Applications of Analysis

- Chloride content is used to assess the suitability of water for drinking, irrigation, and industrial purposes. The permissible limit of chloride content for drinking water is 250 mg/L, according to the World Health Organization (WHO) guidelines.
- Hardness is used to assess the quality of water for domestic, industrial, and agricultural purposes. The desirable limit of hardness for drinking water is 300 mg/L, according to the WHO guidelines.
- Alkalinity is used to control the pH and corrosion of water in various processes, such as water treatment, boiler operation, and wastewater management. The optimum range of alkalinity for drinking water is 100-200 mg/L, according to the WHO guidelines.



# Water

Water is a substance composed of the chemical elements hydrogen and oxygen and existing in gaseous, liquid, and solid states. It is one of the most plentiful and essential of compounds. A tasteless and odourless liquid at room temperature, it has the important ability to dissolve many other substances.

Some of the main properties and characteristics of water are:

- Water is a tiny molecule. It consists of three atoms: two of hydrogen and one of oxygen. Water molecules cling to each other because of a force called hydrogen bonding. It's the reason why water can do amazing things.
- Water is a shape-shifter. It exists in three states on Earth: liquid, gas, and solid. Liquid water is the most common form of water on Earth. Gas water is also called water vapor or steam. Solid water is also called ice or snow.
- Water is the liquid that makes life on Earth possible. As water cycles from the air to the land to the sea and back again, water shapes our planet — and nearly every aspect of our lives. All living things, from tiny cyanobacteria to giant blue whales, need water to survive.
- Water is an inorganic compound with the chemical formula H2O. It is a transparent, tasteless, odorless, and nearly colorless chemical substance, and it is the main constituent of Earth's hydrosphere and the fluids of all known living organisms (in which it acts as a solvent).
- Water has a high specific heat capacity, meaning it can absorb and release a lot of heat without changing its temperature much. This helps regulate the climate and the body temperature of living organisms.
- Water has a high surface tension, meaning it can form droplets and bubbles and resist external forces. This allows water to flow, stick to surfaces, and support some insects and plants on its surface.
- Water has a high latent heat of vaporization, meaning it takes a lot of energy to change water from liquid to gas. This helps cool the environment and the body when water evaporates.
- Water is a universal solvent, meaning it can dissolve many other substances. This makes water essential for chemical reactions, transport of nutrients and wastes, and biological processes.
- Water is a polar molecule, meaning it has a slight positive charge on one end and a slight negative charge on the other. This makes water attract other polar molecules and ions, and form hydrogen bonds with them.

Water is also used for many human activities, such as drinking, cooking, washing, cleaning, agriculture, industry, energy, recreation, and more. Water is a valuable and limited resource that needs to be conserved and protected.



# 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a solution is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. pH = -log[H+]
- The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, lower than 7 being acidic, and higher than 7 being basic. The pH of pure water is 7 at 25°C.
- pH can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper, phenolphthalein, or universal indicator, which change color depending on the acidity or basicity of the solution. pH can also be measured using a pH meter, which is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the force per unit length required to stretch or break the surface of a liquid. Surface tension = F/L
- Surface tension causes liquids to form spherical droplets, to minimize their surface area. It also allows some insects, such as water striders, to walk on water without sinking.
- Surface tension can be measured using a force gauge, which measures the force required to pull a ring or a needle out of the liquid surface. Surface tension can also be measured using a stalagmometer, which measures the number of drops of liquid that fall from a capillary tube under gravity.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a fluid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a fluid. Viscosity = τ/γ
- The shear stress is the force per unit area applied tangentially to the fluid, and the shear rate is the rate of change of velocity per unit distance in the direction perpendicular to the force. Viscosity is also inversely proportional to the fluidity, which is a measure of how easily a fluid flows. Fluidity = 1/viscosity
- Viscosity depends on the temperature, pressure, and composition of the fluid. Generally, viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and decreases with increasing pressure. Viscosity also varies with the type and concentration of solutes or particles in the fluid.
- Viscosity can be measured using a viscometer, which is a device that measures the time required for a known volume of fluid to flow through a narrow tube or a small hole under a constant pressure difference. Viscosity can also be measured using a rheometer, which is a device that measures the torque required to rotate a spindle or a plate in a fluid at a constant angular velocity.



# A Liquid

- A liquid is one of the four states of matter, along with solid, gas, and plasma.
- A liquid is a fluid that has a definite volume but no fixed shape. It can flow and take the shape of its container.
- A liquid is composed of molecules that are loosely bound by intermolecular forces, such as hydrogen bonds or van der Waals forces.
- A liquid can undergo phase transitions to a solid (freezing) or a gas (evaporation or boiling) depending on the temperature and pressure.
- A liquid has some properties that are different from solids and gases, such as:
  - Surface tension: the tendency of a liquid to minimize its surface area and form droplets or bubbles.
  - Viscosity: the resistance of a liquid to flow or deform under stress.
  - Density: the mass per unit volume of a liquid, which usually decreases with increasing temperature.
  - Compressibility: the change in volume of a liquid when subjected to pressure, which is usually small compared to gases.
  - Capillarity: the ability of a liquid to rise or fall in a narrow tube due to the balance of adhesive and cohesive forces.



# 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

- Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, and have a high molecular weight.
- Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, pharmaceuticals, cosmetics, etc.
- Resins can be classified into two main types: thermoplastic and thermosetting.
- Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be softened by heating and hardened by cooling, and can be reshaped repeatedly. Examples of thermoplastic resins are polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, etc.
- Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo irreversible chemical reactions when heated, and form cross-linked networks that cannot be melted or reshaped. Examples of thermosetting resins are phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, epoxy, etc.
- The preparation of different resins depends on the type, source, and properties of the resins, and the desired applications.
- Some common methods of preparing resins are:

  - Polymerization: This is the process of joining small molecules (monomers) to form large molecules (polymers) with repeating units. Polymerization can be initiated by heat, light, catalysts, or other agents. Examples of resins prepared by polymerization are polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, etc.
  - Condensation: This is the process of combining two or more molecules with the elimination of a small molecule, such as water, ammonia, or alcohol. Condensation can be carried out by heating, acid or base catalysis, or other methods. Examples of resins prepared by condensation are phenol-formaldehyde, urea-formaldehyde, epoxy, etc.
  - Esterification: This is the process of reacting an alcohol with an acid to form an ester and water. Esterification can be catalyzed by acids, bases, or enzymes. Examples of resins prepared by esterification are polyester, alkyd, etc.
  - Copolymerization: This is the process of polymerizing two or more different monomers to form a copolymer with a random or regular arrangement of the monomer units. Copolymerization can be controlled by the ratio, reactivity, and sequence of the monomers. Examples of resins prepared by copolymerization are styrene-butadiene, acrylonitrile-butadiene-styrene, etc.
  - Modification: This is the process of altering the properties of a resin by adding or removing functional groups, cross-linking agents, fillers, additives, etc. Modification can be done by chemical or physical means, such as grafting, blending, curing, etc. Examples of resins prepared by modification are silicone, polyurethane, etc.



# Synthesis of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid with the formula C6H10O4. It is mainly used for the production of nylon, polyurethanes, and other polymers.
- The conventional method for the synthesis of adipic acid is the oxidation of a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol (KA oil) with nitric acid . This process involves several steps and generates nitrous oxide (N2O), a greenhouse gas and an ozone-depleting substance.
- The oxidation of KA oil with nitric acid can be carried out in a capillary microreactor system, which offers advantages such as high mass and heat transfer, precise control of reaction conditions, and reduced environmental impact. The reaction kinetics and mechanism of this process have been studied by various researchers.
- Alternatively, adipic acid can be synthesized from renewable sources such as glucose and its derivatives, which are abundant and biodegradable. Several chemical pathways have been proposed for the conversion of glucose to adipic acid, such as oxidation, hydrogenation, dehydration, and decarboxylation. These pathways require the development of efficient and selective nanocatalysts that can overcome the challenges of low yield, high cost, and high energy consumption.



# Acid and Paracetamol by Conventional and Green Route

- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a common painkiller and antipyretic drug that is derived from phenol.
- There are different methods to synthesize paracetamol from phenol, which can be classified as conventional and green routes.
- Conventional routes involve multiple steps, use of hazardous reagents and solvents, and generate by-products and waste.
- Green routes aim to reduce the environmental impact of the synthesis by using fewer steps, safer reagents and solvents, and minimizing by-products and waste.

## Conventional Route

- One of the conventional routes to synthesize paracetamol from phenol is as follows :

1. Nitration of phenol with nitric acid to form p-nitrophenol.
2. Reduction of p-nitrophenol with tin and hydrochloric acid to form p-aminophenol.
3. Acetylation of p-aminophenol with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol.

- The chemical equations for each step are:

Conventional route

- The drawbacks of this route are:

  - It involves three steps, each requiring heating, stirring, and purification.
  - It uses nitric acid, which is corrosive and toxic, and generates nitrous oxide, which is a greenhouse gas.
  - It uses tin, which is expensive and scarce, and generates tin chloride, which is a hazardous waste.
  - It uses acetic anhydride, which is flammable and irritant, and generates acetic acid, which is a waste.

## Green Route

- One of the green routes to synthesize paracetamol from phenol is as follows :

1. Direct amidation of phenol with acetic acid and ammonium acetate in the presence of a zeolite catalyst under microwave irradiation to form paracetamol.

- The chemical equation for this step is:

Green route

- The advantages of this route are:

  - It involves only one step, which is fast and efficient under microwave irradiation.
  - It uses acetic acid and ammonium acetate, which are cheap and biodegradable, and do not generate any waste.
  - It uses a zeolite catalyst, which is reusable and environmentally friendly, and does not leach any metal ions.
  - It does not use any organic solvent, which reduces the risk of fire and pollution.

# LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or demonstrate a known fact. Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables and measuring their effects on other variables.

Some examples of experiments are:

- Testing the effect of different fertilizers on plant growth.
- Measuring the reaction time of humans under different stimuli.
- Comparing the efficiency of different solar panels under different weather conditions.
- Investigating the relationship between temperature and pressure of a gas.
- Determining the pH of different solutions using indicators.
- Observing the behavior of animals in different environments.
- Studying the effect of music on memory and learning.
- Evaluating the performance of different algorithms on a given task.
- Exploring the properties of magnets and electricity.
- Demonstrating the law of conservation of energy using a pendulum.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of verifying the accuracy and precision of an analytical instrument or apparatus by comparing its measurements with known standards or reference materials.
- Calibration ensures that the instrument or apparatus is functioning properly and producing reliable and consistent results.
- Calibration is essential for maintaining the quality and validity of analytical data and for complying with regulatory requirements and quality standards.
- Calibration should be performed periodically, before and after each use, or whenever there is a change in the operating conditions or performance of the instrument or apparatus.
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, and the calibration data should be analyzed and evaluated to determine the accuracy and precision of the instrument or apparatus, and to identify any sources of error or bias.
- Calibration methods vary depending on the type and purpose of the instrument or apparatus, but generally involve the following steps:
  - Selecting appropriate standards or reference materials that have known or certified values of the parameter or property being measured, and that are compatible and traceable to the instrument or apparatus.
  - Preparing and handling the standards or reference materials according to the specified procedures and conditions, and ensuring their stability and integrity.
  - Measuring the standards or reference materials using the instrument or apparatus, and recording the observed values and any relevant information or conditions.
  - Comparing the observed values with the known or certified values of the standards or reference materials, and calculating the deviation, error, uncertainty, or bias of the instrument or apparatus.
  - Adjusting, correcting, or calibrating the instrument or apparatus based on the comparison results, and repeating the measurements until the desired accuracy and precision are achieved.
  - Reporting and documenting the calibration results, and indicating the date, time, conditions, and personnel involved in the calibration process.
  - Labeling and storing the instrument or apparatus properly, and indicating its calibration status and validity period.



# Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Method

- Hardness of water is a measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form complexes with metal ions such as Ca2+ and Mg2+.
- EDTA titration is a method of determining the hardness of water by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of EDTA.
- The indicator used in EDTA titration is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which is a dye that changes color from wine red to blue when it binds to metal ions.
- The pH of the water sample is adjusted to 10 ± 0.1 by adding ammonia buffer, which prevents the precipitation of metal hydroxides and enhances the color change of EBT.
- The end point of the titration is reached when the wine red color of the water sample just turns to blue, indicating that all the metal ions have been complexed by EDTA.

## Procedure for Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Titration

1. Pipette 20 mL of the water sample (V mL) into a 250 mL Erlenmeyer flask.
2. Dilute the sample to about 40 mL by adding distilled water.
3. Add 1 mL of ammonia buffer to adjust the pH to 10 ± 0.1.
4. Add 1 or 2 drops of EBT indicator solution. The sample should turn wine red due to the presence of metal ions.
5. Titrate the sample with a standard EDTA solution of known concentration (C mg/mL) from a burette, with constant shaking, until the color changes from wine red to blue. Record the volume of EDTA used (T mL).
6. Repeat the titration with two more aliquots of the water sample and take the average volume of EDTA used.

## Calculation of Hardness of Water by EDTA Titration

- The hardness of water (H mg/L) can be calculated by the following formula:

  H = (C × T × 1000) / V

  where C is the concentration of EDTA in mg/mL, T is the average volume of EDTA used in mL, and V is the volume of the water sample in mL.

- The hardness of water can be expressed in terms of mg/L of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalent, which is the common unit for water hardness.

- The hardness of water can be classified as soft, moderately hard, hard, or very hard, depending on the concentration of CaCO3 equivalent, as shown in the table below:

| Hardness of water | Concentration of CaCO3 equivalent (mg/L) |
| ----------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| Soft              | < 75                                     |
| Moderately hard   | 75 - 150                                 |
| Hard              | 150 - 300                                |
| Very hard         | > 300                                    |



# Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

- Alkalinity is a measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions in water.
- Alkalinity is important for aquatic life, water quality, and water treatment. It affects the pH, buffering capacity, and corrosion potential of water.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid, and measuring the volume of acid required to reach a specific pH endpoint.
- The endpoint pH depends on the type and concentration of alkalinity present in the water sample. For example, if the water sample contains only bicarbonate alkalinity, the endpoint pH is 4.5. If the water sample contains both bicarbonate and carbonate alkalinity, the endpoint pH is 8.3. If the water sample contains hydroxide alkalinity, the endpoint pH is 10.0.
- The alkalinity of the water sample can be calculated from the volume and concentration of the acid solution, the volume of the water sample, and the stoichiometry of the acid-base reactions.
- The alkalinity of the water sample is usually expressed in milligrams per liter (mg/L) or parts per million (ppm) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalent. This is because calcium carbonate is a common source of alkalinity in natural waters and a standard for water hardness.



# Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte) .
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H3O+] .
- To determine the pH of a solution by titrimetric method, a pH meter can be used to monitor the change in pH as the titrant is added to the analyte  .
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that is sensitive to hydrogen ions and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential. The potential difference between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution .
- A plot of the pH of the solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a titration curve. The titration curve can be used to identify the endpoint or equivalence point of the titration, where the moles of titrant are equal to the moles of analyte .
- The shape and features of the titration curve depend on the type and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration. For example, a strong acid-strong base titration curve has a sharp rise in pH near the equivalence point, while a weak acid-strong base titration curve has a more gradual rise in pH and a buffer region before the equivalence point .
- The titration curve can also be used to calculate the pH at any point of the titration, using the mole balance equation and the equilibrium constants of the acid and base .
- Titrimetric method is a useful and accurate way of determining the pH of a solution, as well as the concentration and identity of the acid and base in the solution .



# 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of a liquid surface that makes it act as if it were a stretched elastic membrane. It is caused by the attraction of the molecules in the surface layer by the bulk of the liquid, which tends to minimize the surface area  .
- Surface tension can be expressed as the amount of force exerted in the surface perpendicular to a line of unit length. The SI unit of surface tension is N/m (newton per meter), and the cgs unit is dyn/cm (dyne per centimeter).
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the bubble pressure method. Each method has its own advantages and limitations, depending on the accuracy, precision, and convenience required.
- The capillary rise method is based on the principle that a liquid will rise or fall in a narrow tube (capillary) depending on the balance of forces between the surface tension, the weight of the liquid, and the atmospheric pressure. The height of the liquid column in the capillary is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid.
- The drop weight method is based on the principle that the weight of a drop of liquid that detaches from a vertical tube (pipette) is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid. The surface tension can be calculated by measuring the weight and the diameter of the drops.
- The ring method is based on the principle that a ring of wire (du Noüy ring) immersed in a liquid and then pulled out will carry a film of liquid whose weight is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid. The surface tension can be calculated by measuring the force required to detach the ring from the liquid surface.
- The bubble pressure method is based on the principle that the pressure inside a bubble of gas (air) blown through a liquid is proportional to the surface tension of the liquid. The surface tension can be calculated by measuring the pressure and the diameter of the bubble.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature and pressure of the fluid.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of the fluid depends on the pressure difference across the tube, the length and radius of the tube, and the viscosity of the fluid.
- The most common type of viscometer is the Ostwald viscometer, which consists of a U-shaped glass tube with two bulbs and a constant diameter capillary connecting them.
- The fluid is filled in the lower bulb and then sucked up to a mark above the upper bulb using a suction pump or a rubber bulb.
- The suction is then released and the time taken for the fluid to flow from the upper mark to the lower mark is recorded.

# Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a light green crystalline salt with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2.6H2O. It is used as a reducing agent in redox titrations.
- Potassium dichromate, K2Cr2O7, is a strong oxidizing agent that can oxidize ferrous ions to ferric ions in acidic medium. It has a bright orange color and forms a green chromium(III) salt after the reaction.
- Potassium ferricyanide, K3[Fe(CN)6], is a red-brown compound that can form a blue precipitate of Prussian blue with ferric ions. It is used as an external indicator in the titration of ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium dichromate.
- The principle of the titration is based on the following redox reaction:

  6Fe2+ + Cr2O7^2- + 14H+ -> 6Fe3+ + 2Cr3+ + 7H2O

- The end point of the titration is detected by adding a drop of potassium ferricyanide solution on a white tile after each addition of potassium dichromate. When the blue color of Prussian blue appears, the titration is complete.
- The procedure of the titration is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium dichromate by dissolving a known mass of the salt in distilled water and making up the volume in a volumetric flask.
  - Pipette out a known volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution into a conical flask and add some dilute sulfuric acid to acidify the solution.
  - Fill a burette with the standard potassium dichromate solution and note the initial reading.
  - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution with the potassium dichromate solution, adding a drop of potassium ferricyanide solution on a white tile after each addition of potassium dichromate.
  - Stop the titration when the blue color of Prussian blue appears on the white tile and note the final burette reading.
  - Repeat the titration for at least two more times and take the average of the concordant readings.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution using the formula:

    N1V1 = N2V2

    where N1 and N2 are the normalities of the ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium dichromate solutions, and V1 and V2 are the volumes of the ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium dichromate solutions used in the titration.



# Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a reducing agent such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is a redox reaction, in which potassium dichromate acts as an oxidizing agent and ferrous ammonium sulphate acts as a reducing agent.
- The reaction can be represented by the following equation:

`6FeSO4(NH4)2SO4.6H2O + K2Cr2O7 + 7H2SO4 -> 3Fe2(SO4)3 + Cr2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + 24H2O + 6NH4`

- The reaction is carried out in an acidic medium, using dilute sulphuric acid as the acid.
- The end point of the reaction can be detected by the change in colour of the solution, from greenish to reddish brown, due to the formation of chromium sulphate.
- The colour change is due to the internal indicator, which is the iron(II) ion present in the ferrous ammonium sulphate solution.
- The iron(II) ion is oxidized to iron(III) ion by the potassium dichromate, and the colour of the iron(III) ion is reddish brown.
- The internal indicator is more reliable than an external indicator, such as diphenylamine sulphonic acid, because it is not affected by the presence of other oxidizing or reducing agents in the solution.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

`Strength of K2Cr2O7 = (Normality of FeSO4(NH4)2SO4.6H2O x Volume of FeSO4(NH4)2SO4.6H2O x Equivalent weight of K2Cr2O7) / Volume of K2Cr2O7`

- The normality of ferrous ammonium sulphate can be determined by titrating it against a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using phenolphthalein as the indicator.
- The equivalent weight of potassium dichromate is 49.03 g.



# Determination of Available Chlorine in Bleaching Powder

- Bleaching powder is a white powder that contains calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2) and calcium chloride (CaCl2) as the main components.
- Bleaching powder is used as a disinfectant, bleaching agent, and deodorizer in various industries and households.
- The effectiveness of bleaching powder depends on the amount of chlorine that can be released when it reacts with water or acids. This is called the available chlorine.
- The available chlorine in bleaching powder can be determined by the iodometric method, which involves the following steps:

  - A known mass of bleaching powder is dissolved in water and filtered to obtain a clear solution.
  - A measured volume of the solution is taken in a conical flask and an excess of potassium iodide (KI) solution is added. This causes the available chlorine to oxidize the iodide ions to iodine (I2) according to the equation:

    Ca(OCl)2 + 4KI + 2HCl -> CaCl2 + 2KCl + 2I2 + 2H2O

  - The liberated iodine is then titrated with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) using starch as an indicator. The starch forms a blue complex with iodine, which disappears at the end point. The equation for the titration is:

    I2 + 2Na2S2O3 -> 2NaI + Na2S4O6

  - The volume of sodium thiosulfate used is noted and the percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder is calculated using the formula:

    % available chlorine = (V x N x 35.5 x 100) / (W x 1000)

    where V is the volume of sodium thiosulfate in mL, N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate, W is the mass of bleaching powder in g, and 35.5 is the equivalent mass of chlorine.



# Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride ions are one of the major inorganic anions in water and wastewater.
- Chloride ions can cause corrosion of metals, affect the taste of water, and increase the salinity of soil and groundwater.
- Chloride ions can be determined by various methods, such as argentometric, potentiometric, colorimetric, and gravimetric methods.
- Argentometric method is based on the precipitation of chloride ions as silver chloride by titrating with a standard silver nitrate solution using a suitable indicator.
- Potentiometric method is based on the measurement of the potential difference between a chloride ion-selective electrode and a reference electrode in a solution containing chloride ions.
- Colorimetric method is based on the formation of a colored complex between chloride ions and a reagent, such as mercuric thiocyanate, ferric thiocyanate, or mercury(II) nitrate, and measuring the absorbance of the solution at a specific wavelength.
- Gravimetric method is based on the precipitation of chloride ions as silver chloride by adding excess silver nitrate solution and filtering, washing, drying, and weighing the precipitate.

## Procedure for argentometric method (Mohr's method)

- Take 50 mL of water sample (V mL) and dilute to 100 mL in a volumetric flask.
- If the sample is highly colored, add 3 mL of aluminum hydroxide and shake well, allow to settle, filter, wash and collect the filtrate.
- Bring the sample pH to 7-8 by adding acid or alkali if necessary.
- Add 1 mL of potassium chromate indicator, which gives a yellow color to the solution.
- Titrate the solution against standard silver nitrate solution (N/50) from a burette until a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate is obtained.
- Note the volume of silver nitrate solution used (V1 mL).

## Calculation

- The reaction between chloride ions and silver nitrate is as follows:

Cl- + Ag+ -> AgCl

- The reaction between chromate ions and silver nitrate is as follows:

2CrO4^2- + 2Ag+ -> Ag2CrO4

- The equivalence point is reached when the amount of silver ions added is equal to the sum of the amount of chloride ions and one-half of the amount of chromate ions in the solution.
- The concentration of chloride ions in the water sample can be calculated by the following formula:

Cl- (mg/L) = (V1 x N x 35.45 x 1000) / V

where,

V1 = volume of silver nitrate solution used (mL)

N = normality of silver nitrate solution

35.45 = equivalent weight of chloride ion

V = volume of water sample taken (mL)

- The chloride content of the water sample can be expressed in terms of mg/L or ppm (parts per million).



# Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde affects the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks.
- The steps involved in the preparation of phenol formaldehyde resin are :

  - Weighing the appropriate quantity of phenol and formaldehyde and mixing them in a reaction vessel.
  - Adding a catalyst, such as hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia, to initiate the polymerization reaction.
  - Heating the mixture to a suitable temperature and controlling the reaction time to achieve the desired degree of polymerization and viscosity.
  - Cooling and neutralizing the resin with an alkali or an acid, depending on the catalyst used.
  - Filtering and drying the resin to remove any impurities and excess water.

- Phenol formaldehyde resin can also be modified by incorporating other monomers, such as urea, melamine, or furfural, to improve its properties and performance.



# Preparation of Urea Formaldehyde (UF) Resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be carried out in one or more steps, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin  .
- The general steps for the preparation of UF resin are as follows:

  - Step 1: Preparation of methylolureas
    - Urea and formaldehyde are mixed in an aqueous solution in a molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11.
    - The mixture is heated to at least 80°C to form methylolureas, which are intermediates that have one or more hydroxymethyl groups attached to the nitrogen atoms of urea .
    - The reaction is exothermic and reversible, and the degree of methylation depends on the temperature, pH, and molar ratio of the reactants .
    - The methylolureas can be isolated by cooling, filtering, and drying, or they can be used directly for the next step.
  - Step 2: Condensation of methylolureas
    - The methylolureas are further reacted with formaldehyde and/or urea under acidic or alkaline conditions to form UF resin  .
    - The pH is adjusted to 0.5-3.5 by adding a mineral or organic acid, such as sulfuric acid, hydrochloric acid, or formic acid .
    - The reaction is continued until the desired viscosity, molecular weight, and formaldehyde content of the resin are achieved  .
    - The reaction is also reversible and can be controlled by the temperature, pH, catalyst, and molar ratio of the reactants  .
    - The UF resin can be isolated by cooling, filtering, and drying, or it can be used directly for the next step.
  - Step 3: Curing of UF resin
    - The UF resin is applied to a substrate, such as wood, paper, or fabric, and cured by heating, pressure, or catalysts to form a cross-linked network .
    - The curing process involves the elimination of water and formaldehyde, and the formation of methylene and dimethylene ether bridges between the urea units .
    - The curing conditions depend on the type and amount of catalyst, the formaldehyde content, the moisture content, and the temperature and time of curing .
    - The cured UF resin has high strength, hardness, and resistance to heat and chemicals, but it also releases formaldehyde, which can cause health and environmental problems .



# Preparation of Adipic acid / Paracetamol

- Adipic acid is the most important dicarboxylic acid, mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Paracetamol is an effective antipyretic and analgesic, widely used for the relief of fever, headaches, and other minor aches and pains.

## Preparation of Adipic acid

- Adipic acid is produced from a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol called KA oil, the abbreviation of ketone-alcohol oil.
- The KA oil is oxidized with nitric acid to give adipic acid, via a multistep pathway.
- The reaction involves the formation of nitrosonium ion (NO+), which attacks the ring and forms a nitroso compound.
- The nitroso compound is further oxidized to a nitro compound, which undergoes ring cleavage and forms a dicarboxylic acid.
- The dicarboxylic acid is then purified by crystallization.

## Preparation of Paracetamol

- Paracetamol is prepared from p-aminophenol by acetylating it with acetic anhydride in the presence of 3-4 drops of concentrated sulphuric acid as catalyst.
- The reaction involves the nucleophilic attack of the amine group on the carbonyl carbon of acetic anhydride, forming an amide bond.
- The acetyl group activates the phenol group, making it more acidic and susceptible to protonation by sulphuric acid.
- The protonated phenol group loses a water molecule, forming a double bond between the oxygen and the carbon.
- The product is then isolated by filtration and recrystallization.



# Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the measure of how well the solution can conduct electric current.
- Cell conductance is the reciprocal of cell resistance, which is the opposition to the flow of electric current in the solution.
- Cell conductance is proportional to the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions in the solution, which are the carriers of electric charge.
- Cell conductance is measured by using a special device called a conductance cell, which consists of two electrodes immersed in the solution and connected to a source of electric potential.
- Cell conductance is also influenced by the geometry and dimensions of the conductance cell, which are expressed by the cell constant.
- Cell constant is the ratio of the distance between the electrodes to the area of cross-section of the electrodes. It has the unit of inverse length (m^-1).
- Cell constant can be determined by measuring the cell resistance of a solution with known conductance, such as a standard solution of potassium chloride (KCl).
- Cell conductance can be calculated from the cell resistance and the cell constant by using the formula:

  Cell conductance = Cell constant / Cell resistance

- Cell conductance can also be expressed as the product of the cell constant and the specific conductance of the solution, which is the conductance of the solution per unit length and unit area.

  Cell conductance = Cell constant x Specific conductance

- Specific conductance is also known as conductivity and has the unit of siemens per meter (S/m).
- Specific conductance depends on the nature and concentration of the electrolyte in the solution, as well as the temperature and pressure of the solution.



# 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Hydrolysis of esters is the reverse reaction of esterification, in which an ester reacts with water to produce a carboxylic acid and an alcohol .
- The hydrolysis of esters can be catalyzed by either an acid or a base. In acidic hydrolysis, the ester is heated with a large excess of water containing a strong-acid catalyst. In basic hydrolysis, the ester is heated with a large excess of water containing a strong-base catalyst .
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on the nature of the ester, the temperature, the pH, and the concentration of the reactants. The rate law for the hydrolysis of esters can be written as:

  - For acidic hydrolysis: `rate = k[ester][H2O]`
  - For basic hydrolysis: `rate = k[ester][OH-]`

  where k is the rate constant and [ ] denotes the concentration of the species .
- The rate constant of hydrolysis of esters can be determined experimentally by measuring the concentration of the ester or one of the products as a function of time. The concentration can be measured by various methods, such as titration, spectrophotometry, conductivity, or gas chromatography .
- One example of an experiment to determine the rate constant of hydrolysis of ethyl acetate by dilute hydrochloric acid is as follows:

  - A known volume and concentration of ethyl acetate is mixed with a large excess of dilute hydrochloric acid and water in a conical flask. The flask is fitted with a reflux condenser and heated to a constant temperature in a water bath .
  - At regular intervals, a small aliquot of the reaction mixture is withdrawn and titrated against a standard solution of sodium hydroxide using phenolphthalein as an indicator. The volume of sodium hydroxide required to reach the end point is recorded .
  - The concentration of ethyl acetate at each time interval can be calculated from the volume of sodium hydroxide used and the stoichiometry of the reaction. The concentration of ethyl acetate can be plotted against time to obtain a curve that shows the decrease in concentration as the reaction proceeds .
  - The rate constant of hydrolysis of ethyl acetate can be obtained from the slope of the curve, using the equation:

    `ln[ester] = -kt + ln[ester]0`

    where [ester] is the concentration of ethyl acetate at time t, [ester]0 is the initial concentration of ethyl acetate, and k is the rate constant .



# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Organic compounds are those that contain carbon atoms, usually bonded to hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, or halogens.
- The structure and properties of organic compounds are largely determined by the functional groups, which are specific groups of atoms or bonds that have characteristic chemical behavior.
- Functional groups can be classified into different categories, such as alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, amines, etc.
- To detect and identify the elements and functional groups in organic compounds, various qualitative and quantitative methods can be used, such as:

## Element detection
- Carbon and hydrogen: These elements are present in almost all organic compounds. They can be detected by heating the compound with copper oxide in a test tube. The carbon will react with oxygen to form carbon dioxide, which can be tested with lime water. The hydrogen will react with oxygen to form water, which can be tested with anhydrous copper sulfate.
- Nitrogen: This element is present in many organic compounds, such as amines, amides, nitriles, etc. It can be detected by the Lassaigne's test, which involves heating the compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The sodium will react with nitrogen to form sodium cyanide, which can be extracted with water and tested with iron(II) sulfate and iron(III) chloride solutions. The formation of Prussian blue color indicates the presence of nitrogen.
- Halogens: These elements are present in many organic compounds, such as alkyl halides, aryl halides, etc. They can be detected by the Beilstein test, which involves heating the compound with a copper wire in a flame. The halogens will react with copper to form volatile copper halides, which impart a characteristic color to the flame. Chlorine gives a green flame, bromine gives a brown flame, and iodine gives a violet flame.
- Sulfur: This element is present in many organic compounds, such as thiols, sulfides, sulfones, etc. It can be detected by the sodium fusion test, similar to the nitrogen detection. The sodium will react with sulfur to form sodium sulfide, which can be extracted with water and tested with lead acetate solution. The formation of a black precipitate of lead sulfide indicates the presence of sulfur.
- Oxygen: This element is present in many organic compounds, such as alcohols, ethers, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, etc. It can be detected by the sodium fusion test, similar to the nitrogen and sulfur detection. The sodium will react with oxygen to form sodium oxide, which can be extracted with water and tested with phenolphthalein indicator. The formation of a pink color indicates the presence of oxygen.

Primary alcohols give turbidity after several minutes, secondary alcohols give turbidity within a few minutes, and tertiary alcohols give turbidity immediately.
  - Esterification test: This test involves heating the compound with acetic acid and a few drops of concentrated sulfuric acid. The formation of a fruity smell indicates the presence of an alcoholic -OH group. The smell is due to the formation of an ester, which is a compound with the general formula RCOOR'.
- Carbonyls (Aldehydes and Ketones): These functional groups are present in compounds such as aldehydes, ketones, etc. They can be detected by the following tests:
  - 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine test: This test involves adding a small amount of the compound to a solution of 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine in ethanol and sulfuric acid. The formation of a yellow, orange, or red precipitate indicates the presence of a carbonyl group. The precipitate is a hydrazone, which is a compound with the general formula R-C=N-NH-R'.
  - Tollen's test: This test involves adding a few drops of the compound to a solution of silver nitrate in ammonia. The formation of a silver mirror on

# Level

- A level is a tool or device that is used to measure or indicate whether a surface is horizontal (level) or vertical (plumb).
- A level consists of a horizontal tube or vial that contains a liquid (usually alcohol or water) and an air bubble. The position of the bubble indicates the alignment of the surface relative to the earth's gravity.
- A level can be used for various purposes, such as construction, carpentry, engineering, surveying, landscaping, photography, etc.
- There are different types of levels, such as spirit levels, laser levels, digital levels, water levels, etc. Each type has its own advantages and disadvantages depending on the application and accuracy required.
- A level can be calibrated by placing it on a flat surface and adjusting it until the bubble is centered in the vial. Then, the level is rotated 180 degrees and placed on the same surface. If the bubble is still centered, the level is accurate. If not, the level needs to be adjusted or replaced.

- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on the principle or technique of analysis, such as spectroscopy, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, thermal analysis, separation analysis, microscopy, etc .
- Analytical instruments have various applications in different fields, such as pharmaceutical, food-processing, chemical, clinical, environmental, forensic, life science, etc .
- Analytical instruments can help in determining the composition, structure, function, quality, purity, safety, or performance of a sample or a substance .
- Analytical instruments can also help in solving problems, developing new methods, validating results, or monitoring processes .

- Analytical instruments can be operated manually or automatically, depending on the level of automation, control, and data processing of the instrument.



# CO2: Molecular and System Properties

## Molecular Properties

- CO2 is a linear molecule with a bond angle of 180 degrees and a bond length of 116.3 pm.
- CO2 has a molecular weight of 44.0095 g/mol and a molar volume of 22.26 L/mol at STP.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero and a quadrupole moment of 4.67 D Å.
- CO2 has a vibrational frequency of 1333 cm-1 for the symmetric stretching mode and 2349 cm-1 for the asymmetric stretching mode.
- CO2 has a rotational constant of 0.3902 cm-1 and a centrifugal distortion constant of 1.35 x 10-6 cm-1.

## System Properties

- CO2 is a gas at standard conditions, but can be liquefied or solidified under high pressure or low temperature.
- CO2 has a critical point at 304.25 K and 73.8 bar, and a triple point at 216.58 K and 5.185 bar.
- CO2 has a density of 1.977 kg/m3 at STP, and a specific weight of 19.41 N/m3 at STP.
- CO2 has a surface tension of 0.0126 N/m at the triple point, and decreases with increasing temperature and pressure .
- CO2 has a viscosity of 0.0148 mPa s at STP, and increases with increasing pressure and decreases with increasing temperature.
- CO2 has a thermal conductivity of 0.0166 W/m K at STP, and increases with increasing temperature and pressure.
- CO2 has a specific heat capacity of 0.844 kJ/kg K at STP, and varies with temperature and pressure.
- CO2 has a latent heat of vaporization of 200 kJ/kg at the triple point, and decreases with increasing temperature and pressure.
- CO2 has a latent heat of fusion of 184 kJ/kg at the triple point, and decreases with increasing pressure.



# Viscosity, Conductance of Solution, Chloride and Iron Content in Water

## Viscosity
- Viscosity is the property of a fluid that measures its resistance to flow.
- Viscosity depends on the internal friction between the molecules of the fluid, which is affected by factors such as temperature, pressure, and composition.
- Viscosity is expressed by the formula:

    `η = F / (A * dv / dy)`

    where η is the viscosity, F is the force required to move a layer of fluid, A is the area of the layer, dv / dy is the velocity gradient between the layers.
- The SI unit of viscosity is pascal-second (Pa·s), which is equivalent to newton-second per square meter (N·s/m²) or kilogram per meter per second (kg/m·s).
- Some examples of fluids with different viscosities are:

    | Fluid | Viscosity (Pa·s) at 20°C |
    |-------|-------------------------|
    | Water | 0.001                   |
    | Honey | 10                      |
    | Tar   | 1000                    |

- Viscosity can be classified into two types: dynamic viscosity and kinematic viscosity.
- Dynamic viscosity is the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate of a fluid, and it measures the force required to move a layer of fluid.
- Kinematic viscosity is the ratio of the dynamic viscosity to the density of a fluid, and it measures the resistance to flow of a fluid under the influence of gravity.
- The SI unit of kinematic viscosity is square meter per second (m²/s), which is equivalent to stoke (St) or centistoke (cSt).
- Some examples of fluids with different kinematic viscosities are:

    | Fluid | Kinematic Viscosity (m²/s) at 20°C |
    |-------|-----------------------------------|
    | Water | 0.000001                          |
    | Honey | 0.01                              |
    | Tar   | 10                                |

## Conductance of Solution
- Conductance of solution is the ability of a solution to conduct electric current.
- Conductance of solution depends on the concentration, type, and mobility of the ions present in the solution, as well as the temperature and pressure of the solution.
- Conductance of solution is expressed by the formula:

    `G = I / V`

    where G is the conductance, I is the current, and V is the potential difference across the solution.
- The SI unit of conductance is siemens (S), which is equivalent to ampere per volt (A/V) or ohm inverse (Ω⁻¹).
- Some examples of solutions with different conductances are:

    | Solution | Concentration (mol/L) | Conductance (S/m) at 25°C |
    |----------|-----------------------|--------------------------|
    | NaCl     | 0.01                  | 0.2                      |
    | KCl      | 0.01                  | 0.15                     |
    | HCl      | 0.01                  | 0.4                      |

- Conductance of solution can be classified into two types: specific conductance and equivalent conductance.
- Specific conductance is the conductance of a unit volume of a solution, and it measures the conductivity of the solution.
- Equivalent conductance is the conductance of a solution containing one gram-equivalent of the solute, and it measures the contribution of each ion to the conductivity of the solution.
- The SI unit of specific conductance is siemens per meter (S/m), which is equivalent to ohm inverse per meter (Ω⁻¹/m).
- The SI unit of equivalent conductance is siemens per meter squared per mole (S·m²/mol), which is equivalent to ohm inverse per meter squared per mole (Ω⁻¹·m²/mol).
- Some examples of solutions with different specific and equivalent conductances are:

    | Solution | Concentration (mol/L) | Specific Conductance (S/m) at 25°C | Equivalent Conductance (S·m²/mol) at 25°C |
    |----------|-----------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
    | NaCl     | 0.01                  | 0.2                               | 2000                                     |
    | KCl      | 0.01                  | 0.15                              | 1500                                     |
    | HCl      | 0.01                  | 0.

# CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness of water is the measure of the amount of calcium and magnesium salts dissolved in it. It is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3) concentration in mg/L or ppm.
- Alkalinity of water is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium and other metals. It is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3) concentration in mg/L or ppm.
- Hardness and alkalinity of water are important parameters for water quality assessment, as they affect the suitability of water for domestic, industrial and agricultural purposes.
- Hardness and alkalinity of water can be measured by titration methods using standard solutions of acids and bases, and suitable indicators.
- The common methods for measuring hardness and alkalinity of water are:
  - EDTA method for total hardness and calcium hardness
  - Soap method for total hardness
  - Phenolphthalein and methyl orange methods for total alkalinity and phenolphthalein alkalinity
  - Borax method for total alkalinity
  - Hydroxylamine hydrochloride method for carbonate and bicarbonate alkalinity
  - Potentiometric method for total alkalinity and carbonate alkalinity
- The steps involved in each method are:
  - EDTA method: A known volume of water sample is taken in a conical flask and a few drops of Eriochrome Black T indicator are added. The solution turns wine red due to the formation of a complex between the indicator and the metal ions. The solution is then titrated with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) until the end point is reached, indicated by a color change from wine red to blue. The volume of EDTA used is noted and the total hardness is calculated. For calcium hardness, the same procedure is repeated after adding a buffer solution of ammonia and ammonium chloride, which masks the magnesium ions. The difference between the total hardness and the calcium hardness gives the magnesium hardness.
  - Soap method: A known volume of water sample is taken in a stoppered bottle and a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator are added. If the solution turns pink, it indicates the presence of alkalinity and a few drops of sulfuric acid are added to neutralize it. Then, a standard solution of soap is added drop by drop with shaking until a lather is formed that persists for at least 5 minutes. The volume of soap solution used is noted and the total hardness is calculated.
  - Phenolphthalein and methyl orange methods: A known volume of water sample is taken in a conical flask and a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator are added. If the solution turns pink, it indicates the presence of phenolphthalein alkalinity, which is due to the hydroxides and half of the carbonates. The solution is then titrated with a standard solution of sulfuric acid until the end point is reached, indicated by a color change from pink to colorless. The volume of acid used is noted and the phenolphthalein alkalinity is calculated. Then, a few drops of methyl orange indicator are added to the same solution, which turns yellow. The solution is further titrated with the same acid until the end point is reached, indicated by a color change from yellow to orange. The volume of acid used is noted and the total alkalinity is calculated. The difference between the total alkalinity and the phenolphthalein alkalinity gives the methyl orange alkalinity, which is due to the bicarbonates and the remaining carbonates.
  - Borax method: A known volume of water sample is taken in a conical flask and a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator are added. If the solution turns pink, it indicates the presence of alkalinity and a few drops of sulfuric acid are added to neutralize it. Then, a standard solution of sodium tetraborate (borax) is added with constant stirring until the end point is reached, indicated by a color change from colorless to pink. The volume of borax used is noted and the total alkalinity is calculated.
  - Hydroxylamine hydrochloride method: A known volume of water sample is taken in a conical flask and a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator are added. If the solution turns pink, it indicates the presence of alkalinity and a few drops of sulfuric acid are added to neutralize it. Then, a known excess of hydroxylamine hydrochloride solution is added, which reacts with the carbonates



# CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound with the formula C6H5OH. It has a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. Phenol is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol is widely used as an antiseptic, disinfectant, and precursor for many industrial chemicals.

Some of the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol are:

- Phenol can be prepared from benzene by electrophilic aromatic substitution with a mixture of concentrated sulfuric acid and nitric acid. This reaction produces nitrobenzene, which can be reduced to aniline by hydrogenation or by using a reducing agent such as tin and hydrochloric acid. Aniline can then be converted to phenol by diazotization with nitrous acid and hydrolysis with water.

- Phenol can also be prepared from benzene by direct hydroxylation with oxygen in the presence of a catalyst such as vanadium pentoxide (V2O5) or iron oxide (Fe2O3). This reaction is known as the Dow process and produces phenol and acetone as the main products.

- Phenol can be prepared from coal tar, which is a by-product of the destructive distillation of coal. Coal tar contains various aromatic compounds, including benzene, toluene, xylene, naphthalene, and phenol. Phenol can be separated from coal tar by fractional distillation, solvent extraction, or crystallization.

- Phenol can be prepared from cumene, which is an alkylbenzene with the formula C6H5CH(CH3)2. Cumene can be oxidized by air to produce cumene hydroperoxide, which can be cleaved by acid to yield phenol and acetone. This reaction is known as the cumene process and is the most widely used industrial method for the production of phenol.

- Phenol can be prepared from chlorobenzene, which is an aromatic compound with the formula C6H5Cl. Chlorobenzene can be hydrolyzed by sodium hydroxide (NaOH) at high temperature and pressure to produce phenol and sodium chloride (NaCl). This reaction is known as the Dow-Henkel process and is used to produce phenol from waste chlorobenzene.



# Formaldehyde

- Formaldehyde is a naturally occurring organic compound with the formula CH2O and structure H−CHO.
- Formaldehyde is a colorless, highly toxic, and flammable gas at room temperature.
- Formaldehyde is an intermediate in the oxidation (or combustion) of methane, as well as of other carbon compounds, e.g. in forest fires, automobile exhaust, and tobacco smoke.
- Formaldehyde is also produced in the atmosphere by the action of sunlight and oxygen on atmospheric methane and other hydrocarbons, and it becomes part of smog.
- Formaldehyde is used widely by industry to manufacture building materials and numerous household products, such as fertilizer, paper, plywood, resins, antiseptics, medicines, and cosmetics .
- Formaldehyde is also used as a food preservative and in embalming fluids.
- Formaldehyde can cause health effects, such as eye, nose, and throat irritation, respiratory problems, skin rashes, and cancer .
- Formaldehyde exposure can occur from inhalation, ingestion, or skin contact with products or materials that contain or release formaldehyde .
- Formaldehyde levels in indoor air can vary depending on temperature, humidity, ventilation, and the use of formaldehyde-containing products.
- Formaldehyde levels can be reduced by using low-emitting products, increasing ventilation, and maintaining moderate temperature and humidity.



# K3

- K3 is a type of visa that allows a foreign spouse of a U.S. citizen to enter and reside in the United States while waiting for the approval of their immigrant visa petition.
- K3 visa is a nonimmigrant visa, which means it does not grant permanent residence or a green card. However, K3 visa holders can apply for adjustment of status to become permanent residents after their immigrant visa petition is approved.
- K3 visa is valid for two years and can be renewed if the immigrant visa petition or the adjustment of status application is still pending.
- K3 visa holders can work and study in the United States with the appropriate authorization from the U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS).
- K3 visa holders can also travel outside the United States and re-enter with a valid visa and passport.
- To be eligible for a K3 visa, the foreign spouse must meet the following requirements:
  - Be legally married to a U.S. citizen.
  - Have a pending Form I-130, Petition for Alien Relative, filed by the U.S. citizen spouse with the USCIS.
  - Have a Form I-129F, Petition for Alien Fiancé(e), filed by the U.S. citizen spouse with the USCIS and forwarded to the National Visa Center (NVC).
  - Have a valid passport and a medical examination.
  - Have no criminal or immigration violations that would make them inadmissible to the United States.
  - Have sufficient financial support from the U.S. citizen spouse or a joint sponsor.
  - Have a valid relationship with the U.S. citizen spouse and intend to live together as spouses in the United States.



# CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction K3

- The rate constant of a reaction is a proportionality factor that relates the reaction rate to the concentrations of the reactants.
- The rate constant depends on the temperature, the activation energy, and the frequency factor of the reaction.
- The rate constant can have different units depending on the order of the reaction.
- For a termolecular reaction, such as A + B + C → products, the reaction rate is described by r=k3[A][B][C], where k3 is a termolecular rate constant.
- To estimate the rate constant of a termolecular reaction, one can use the Arrhenius equation: k3=Ae^(-Ea/RT), where A is the frequency factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.
- Alternatively, one can use experimental data to plot the natural logarithm of the rate constant versus the inverse of the temperature, and obtain the slope and the intercept of the linear regression. The slope is equal to -Ea/R and the intercept is equal to ln(A).
- If the reaction takes place in multiple steps, the overall rate constant can be expressed as a function of the individual rate constants of each step .
- For example, if the reaction is A + B + C → products, and it takes place in three steps with rate constants k1, k2, and k3, the overall rate constant k can be calculated as k=k2k1k3/(k1+k2+k3).
- The individual rate constants of each step can be estimated using the Arrhenius equation or experimental data, as described above.

# Page 32 of 40

- This page covers the topic of **linear regression**, which is a statistical method for modeling the relationship between a dependent variable and one or more independent variables.
- Linear regression assumes that the dependent variable can be expressed as a linear combination of the independent variables, plus some random error term.
- The general form of a linear regression model is:

  `y = b0 + b1x1 + b2x2 + ... + bnxn + e`

  where:

  - `y` is the dependent variable
  - `x1, x2, ..., xn` are the independent variables
  - `b0, b1, b2, ..., bn` are the regression coefficients
  - `e` is the error term

- The goal of linear regression is to estimate the regression coefficients that minimize the sum of squared errors (SSE) between the observed and predicted values of the dependent variable.
- The most common method for estimating the regression coefficients is the **ordinary least squares (OLS)** method, which solves a system of normal equations to obtain the best fit line.
- The OLS method has some desirable properties, such as:

  - It is unbiased, meaning that the expected value of the estimated coefficients is equal to the true value of the coefficients.
  - It is consistent, meaning that as the sample size increases, the estimated coefficients converge to the true value of the coefficients.
  - It is efficient, meaning that it has the smallest variance among all unbiased estimators of the coefficients.

- However, the OLS method also has some limitations, such as:

  - It requires the independent variables to be linearly independent, meaning that none of them can be expressed as a linear combination of the others.
  - It requires the error term to be normally distributed, with a constant variance and zero mean, and to be independent of the independent variables.
  - It is sensitive to outliers, meaning that a few extreme observations can have a large influence on the estimated coefficients and the fit of the model.

- To overcome some of these limitations, there are other methods for estimating the regression coefficients, such as:

  - **Weighted least squares (WLS)**, which assigns different weights to the observations based on their reliability or importance, and minimizes the weighted sum of squared errors.
  - **Generalized least squares (GLS)**, which accounts for the heteroscedasticity (non-constant variance) or autocorrelation (non-independence) of the error term, and transforms the model to make the error term homoscedastic and independent.
  - **Ridge regression**, which adds a penalty term to the SSE that shrinks the coefficients towards zero, and reduces the multicollinearity (high correlation) among the independent variables.
  - **Lasso regression**, which also adds a penalty term to the SSE that shrinks the coefficients towards zero, but also sets some of them to exactly zero, and performs variable selection.
  - **Elastic net regression**, which combines the penalty terms of ridge and lasso regression, and balances the trade-off between shrinking and selecting the coefficients.

- To evaluate the performance of a linear regression model, there are several metrics that can be used, such as:

  - **Coefficient of determination (R-squared)**, which measures the proportion of the variance in the dependent variable that is explained by the independent variables, and ranges from 0 to 1, with higher values indicating a better fit.
  - **Adjusted R-squared**, which adjusts the R-squared for the number of independent variables in the model, and penalizes the addition of irrelevant variables, and ranges from 0 to 1, with higher values indicating a better fit.
  - **Root mean squared error (RMSE)**, which measures the average deviation of the predicted values from the observed values, and has the same units as the dependent variable, with lower values indicating a better fit.
  - **Mean absolute error (MAE)**, which measures the average absolute deviation of the predicted values from the observed values, and has the same units as the dependent variable, with lower values indicating a better fit.
  - **Mean absolute percentage error (MAPE)**, which measures the average relative deviation of the predicted values from the observed values, and is expressed as a percentage, with lower values indicating a better fit.

- To test the significance of the regression coefficients, there are several methods that can be used, such as:

  - **t-test**, which tests the null
